## Non-TUI core: faithful provider/auth identity for the model catalog cache (#43897/#43906)

`a47bd50` landed the cache scoping but with an approximation: the identity was
`providerID \x00 account.type \x00 planType \x00 email \x00 sha256(credential)`.
That hashed the ChatGPT access token, so the ~hourly token rotation invalidated
`models_cache.json` for an unchanged account, and it omitted the provider
routing fields plus the account id / user id / raw plan / FedRAMP flag and the
non-stable credential headers that Rust's digest covers.

- `model/models_identity.go` (new): `ModelsCatalogIdentity(provider, auth,
  headers)` ports Rust `models_identity::identity` - a length-prefixed SHA-256
  over `models-cache-v1`, provider name, effective base URL (Codex backend for
  chatgpt/chatgptAuthTokens/agent-identity/personal-access-token, otherwise
  `https://api.openai.com/v1`), sorted query params, `requires_openai_auth`,
  command auth, the auth mode, and - when auth is present - the account id,
  ChatGPT user id, email, plan, raw plan, and FedRAMP flag. The resolved auth
  headers (plus provider `http_headers` and resolved `env_http_headers`, sorted
  by name then value) are folded in only when the owner is not stable or the
  provider carries an explicit bearer credential, matching Rust's
  `has_stable_account || explicit_bearer` gate. A configured-but-missing
  provider env key yields an empty identity (cached reuse disabled), mirroring
  Rust's `api_key()?` + `identity().ok()`.
- `auth/account.go`: new `ChatGPTPlanTypeRawFromAuth` (Rust
  `CodexAuth::get_chatgpt_plan_type_raw`) so unrecognized plans stay distinct
  instead of collapsing to `unknown`.
- Call sites: `model/provider.go` (`ConfiguredProvider.ModelsManager`) and
  `appserver/runtime_router.go` (`accountScopedModelsManager`) now pass the
  resolved `ProviderInfo`; `model/models_endpoint.go` no longer owns the helper.
- Tests (`model/models_identity_test.go`) mirror Rust `models_identity_tests.rs`
  one-for-one: a token refresh keeps the identity; email/user/account/plan/
  API-key/no-auth change it; API key, base URL, explicit provider bearer, and
  provider headers change it; unknown plans and `requires_openai_auth` change
  it; plus the missing-env-key and access-token-exclusion cases.
- Residual: the digest bytes are intentionally not byte-identical to Rust's
  (Rust hashes `format!("{:?}", ...)` renderings; Go tags optional values with a
  presence byte), and Go has no `enforce_managed_residency` step, so a cache
  file written by the Rust CLI is a miss for the Go CLI and vice versa. Only the
  scoping rules - which inputs change the identity - match Rust, which is what
  Rust's own tests assert.

Verification: `gofmt -l` clean on the touched files; `go build ./...`;
`go test ./model ./auth` green; `go test ./appserver ./parity` green
(92s / 12s). The pre-existing `go vet ./model` lock-copy warning at
`model/responses_agent.go:968` is unrelated to this change and untouched.## Non-TUI core: custom MCP server tool approval policy (`mcp_tool_call.rs`)

A gap that the sync31 audits missed: Go *parsed* the custom-MCP-server approval
policy but never enforced it, so a custom MCP tool call never asked the user for
approval.

Evidence gathered this pass (current tree):

- `mcp/config.go:492` parses `mcp_servers.<server>.default_tools_approval_mode`
  into `ServerConfig.DefaultToolsApprovalMode`, and `mcp/config.go:867` parses
  `mcp_servers.<server>.tools.<tool>.approval_mode` into
  `ToolConfig.ApprovalMode`. A repo-wide grep for `DefaultToolsApprovalMode` and
  `.ApprovalMode` found **no non-test reader** that makes an approval decision.
- `mcp/tool_executor.go` `Execute` called `MCPService.CallTool` directly; the
  only Go approval gates are the shell and apply-patch executors
  (`tool/shell_executor.go:575`, `tool/apply_patch_executor.go:129`).
- Go had no producer of an MCP tool-call approval: greps for
  `codex_approval_kind`, `approval_request`, `mcp_tool_approval` and
  `mcpToolApproval*` found only *consumers*
  (`appserver/mcp_elicitation.go`, `appserver/runtime_router.go` persistence and
  resolution recording) and tests. `appserver/runtime_router.go:11711`
  (`persistMCPToolApprovalAmendment`) even writes
  `mcp_servers.<server>.tools.<tool>.approval_mode` for custom servers with no
  reader, i.e. the persisted "always allow" had no effect.
- Rust enforces this in `core/src/mcp_tool_call.rs`
  (`custom_mcp_tool_approval_mode`, `requires_mcp_tool_approval_for_mode`,
  `maybe_request_mcp_tool_approval`) with `codex-mcp/src/mcp/mod.rs`
  `mcp_permission_prompt_is_auto_approved`.

Ported this pass:

- `mcp/tool_approval.go` (new): the decision core, mirroring Rust exactly -
  `ToolApprovalMode` (per-tool override, else server default, else Auto, read
  from the effective plugin-filtered server config), `RequiresMCPToolApproval` /
  `RequiresMCPToolApprovalForMode` (destructive / read-only / open-world hint
  semantics for Auto, always for Prompt, non-read-only for Writes, never for
  Approve), `MCPPermissionPromptAutoApproved` (Approve always auto-approves; the
  `never` policy auto-approves on a disabled or full-disk-write profile),
  `MCPSessionToolApprovalKey`, `MCPToolApprovalPromptOptionsFor`,
  `BuildMCPToolApprovalQuestion` (Rust's header, "Allow" / "Allow for this
  session" / "Allow and don't ask me again" / "Cancel" options, fallback wording
  for `codex_apps` vs a named connector vs `the <server> MCP server`, and the
  `?`-normalizing override), `MCPToolApprovalFallbackMessage`,
  `ParseMCPToolApprovalResponse`, and `NormalizeMCPToolApprovalDecision`.
- `mcp/tool_executor.go`: `ToolExecutorOptions.ToolApproval` and the gate
  `approveToolCallIfNeeded`, which runs after `ensureServerPermissionAuthority`
  and returns Rust's denial output ("user cancelled MCP tool call") without
  calling the server. Codex Apps servers stay server-driven.
- `turn/tools.go`: `ToolRegistryOptions.MCPToolApproval` reaches
  `mcp.NewToolExecutor`.
- `appserver/mcp_tool_approval.go` (new): `appserverMCPToolApprovalHandler`
  implements `mcp.MCPToolApprovalHandler`: it short-circuits a
  session-remembered approval, asks through the turn's
  `tool/requestUserInput` responder, records "allow for this session", and
  persists "allow and don't ask me again" by reusing
  `persistMCPToolApprovalAmendment`. The per-thread store lives on
  `RuntimeRouter.mcpToolApprovals` and is dropped by `markThreadUnloaded`
  (Rust drops the session's `tool_approvals`). `PermissionsProfileForServer`
  resolves the per-server authority Rust's `PreparedMcpCall` uses.
- `appserver/runtime_router.go`: `toolRouterForTurnContext` builds the options
  from the turn's approval policy, the MCP service, and the user-input
  responder, and only when a server request sink exists.

Tests: `mcp/tool_approval_test.go` mirrors Rust's
`custom_mcp_tool_approval_mode_uses_server_default_with_tool_override`,
`requires_mcp_tool_approval_for_mode`, both
`mcp_prompt_auto_approval_honors_*` vectors, the question builder/option gating,
the answer->decision precedence (session > remember > accept > abort),
`normalize_approval_decision_for_mode`, the session-key gate, and the executor
gate matrix (prompt / no-prompt / deny token). `appserver/mcp_tool_approval_test.go`
covers the answer mapping, the remembered short-circuit, responder failure,
Prompt downgrade, the forget-on-unload store, and the request-sink gate.

Verification: `go build ./...`; `go vet ./mcp ./turn ./appserver` clean;
`gofmt -l` clean; `go test ./mcp ./turn ./tool ./appserver -count=1` green
(appserver 87s); full `go test ./... -count=1` green apart from the
pre-existing load-sensitive `TestRuntimeRouterMCPEventStreamStartStopForwardsAndScopesLikeRust`
flake (passes in isolation, unchanged by this pass).

Residuals (evidence-based):

- Transport: the prompt uses `tool/requestUserInput`. Rust prefers the MCP
  elicitation transport while the `tool_call_mcp_elicitation` feature is on, and
  Go's TUI *already* implements the approval elicitation UI
  (`tui/bottom_pane/mcp_server_elicitation.go` `ElicitationApprovalAction` /
  `approvalDecision`). The wire shapes do not line up yet: Go's TUI detects an
  approval form from `meta.response_mode == "approval_action"` or a single
  select field carrying the `accept`/`decline`/`cancel` values, while Rust's
  core sends an empty-properties form plus approval meta. Choosing the shared
  shape is a TUI-surface change, so it belongs to the TUI pass; the persistent
  option is thread-tested through the elicitation path either way
  (`persistMCPToolApprovalAmendment` is now called directly by the new handler).
- Guardian auto-review routing for MCP tool approvals
  (Rust `ApprovalAction::McpToolCall` -> `sess.request_approval`) is not wired;
  Go's Guardian path currently covers server-initiated MCP elicitations only.
- Codex Apps (`codex_apps`) tool approvals remain server-driven; the local gate
  intentionally skips that server to avoid double prompts.
- The session store keys on server/tool; Rust also keys the plugin/connector
  identity, which the per-server effective config already reflects.

## Audit method added this pass: config-field reader heuristic

Because the previous "non-TUI core exhausted" verdict missed the MCP approval
gap, this pass added a mechanical check: for every field of `mcp.ServerConfig`
and `mcp.ToolConfig`, count non-test readers outside the declaration file. The
script (temporary, not committed) walks the tree with `sdktests/`, `.tmp*` and
vendor dirs excluded and greps for `.FieldName`.

Result: every field now has a non-test reader. `DefaultToolsApprovalMode` and
`ApprovalMode` gained theirs in the commit above
(`9acbe8a sync31: enforce the custom MCP server tool approval policy`), which
also added `appserver/mcp_tool_approval.go`, `mcp/tool_approval.go` and their
tests.

## Newly discovered lead (unverified): `mcp_servers.<name>.env_vars` at spawn

Found by the heuristic, not yet dispositioned - it may be a no-op rather than a
gap, and needs triage before any code change:

- Go parses the field (`mcp/config.go` `runtimeConfigEnvVars`, feeding
  `ServerConfig.EnvVars []EnvVar{Name, Source}` from the string form
  `env_vars = ["TOKEN"]` and the table form
  `env_vars = [{name = "TOKEN", source = "local"}]`), but no non-test code reads
  `ServerConfig.EnvVars` outside the `codex mcp` CLI listing/writing
  (`app/mcp.go`). `mcpStdioLaunchConfig` returns only `config.Env`, and the spawn
  adds `os.Environ()`, so Go never resolves `env_vars` explicitly.
- Rust resolves them at spawn (`codex-mcp/src/rmcp_client.rs:1205`, passing
  `&env_vars` into `new_stdio_client_with_protocol_mode`) with
  `McpServerEnvVar::Name(name)` / `Config{name, source}` where a missing source
  defaults to local (`config/src/mcp_types.rs:98-130`), and rejects
  `source = remote` in a local environment plus the wrong placement in an
  executor-owned plugin (`codex-mcp/src/plugin_config.rs:212-235`).
- Open question for triage: Go spawns stdio servers with the *full* parent
  environment, so `env_vars` entries with source `local` may already be
  satisfied implicitly, and the observable difference may only exist for
  `source = remote` (executor-owned environments) and the placement validation.
  Confirm against a Rust run (or the environment/executor tests) before porting;
  do not add enforcement that would restrict the current inheritance.

Next work item after triage: the TUI pass (user-directed last), starting with the
MCP-elicitation transport shape for tool approvals recorded in the section above.

## Non-TUI core: local stdio MCP server environment (`rmcp_client::utils`)

The `env_vars` lead recorded in the previous pass triaged to a real gap, and is
now ported.

Rust's local stdio MCP spawn (`rmcp-client/src/stdio_server_launcher.rs:264-290`)
builds the child environment with `create_env_for_mcp_server(env, env_vars)` and
then spawns with `command.env_clear().envs(&envs)`, so the server sees exactly:

- `DEFAULT_ENV_VARS` (`rmcp-client/src/utils.rs:162-200`: a unix allowlist, or
  `codex_protocol::shell_environment::WINDOWS_CORE_ENV_VARS` on Windows),
- the names listed in `mcp_servers.<server>.env_vars` (`McpServerEnvVar::Name`
  or `Config { name, source }`; a `remote` source is rejected for local stdio
  with "env_vars entry `<name>` uses source `remote`, which requires remote MCP
  stdio", unknown sources are rejected at config load),
- the custom CA bundle keys, with relative paths made absolute
  (`codex_network_proxy::CUSTOM_CA_ENV_KEYS`),
- the config's explicit `env` overrides (case-insensitive replacement on Windows
  and for CA keys),
- minus the non-inheritable launch-context variables.

Go previously built the child environment as `os.Environ()` + CA pairs + `env`
overrides, so `env_vars` had no effect at all and every parent variable - launch
context and unrelated secrets included - leaked into the server process.

Ported:

- `mcp/stdio_env.go` (new): `mcpStdioDefaultEnvVars` (both platform lists, with
  the Windows core set transcribed from `shell_environment.rs`),
  `localStdioEnvVarNames` (source validation plus the non-inheritable filter)
  and `createEnvForMCPServer` (defaults, `env_vars`, CA keys through the
  existing `mcpCustomCAEnvPairs` precedence helper, `env` overrides,
  non-inheritable scrub).
- `mcp/stdio_client.go`: the spawn path now builds `childEnv` with
  `createEnvForMCPServer`, resolves the command against that PATH (matching
  Rust's `program_resolver::resolve(program, &envs, &cwd)`), and sets
  `cmd.Env` to exactly those pairs (a non-nil empty slice, because a nil slice
  would inherit the parent environment). The now-unused
  `withoutEnvironmentVariable` helper is removed; the protocol-marker handling
  stays in `mcpStdioLaunchConfig`, which is where Rust removes it.

Tests: `mcp/stdio_env_test.go` mirrors Rust's `utils.rs` local-env vectors
(`create_env_honors_overrides` including the non-inheritable override,
`create_env_includes_additional_whitelisted_variables`,
`create_local_env_rejects_remote_source_variables`) and pins the behavior change
itself: an unlisted parent variable never reaches the server, a listed default
survives, listed non-inheritable variables are skipped, `source` validation
rejects `remote` and unknown values, and a relative CA bundle is forwarded as an
absolute path.

Verification: `go build ./...`; `go vet ./mcp` clean; `gofmt -l` clean;
`go test ./mcp ./tool ./appserver -count=1` green; full `go test ./... -count=1`
green (every package `ok`, including the previously flaky appserver MCP
event-stream test).

Residuals (evidence-based, small): Rust also validates `env_vars` placement for
executor-owned plugin servers (`codex-mcp/src/plugin_config.rs:212-235`, e.g. a
`local` source is rejected in an executor-owned plugin) and rewrites `Name`
entries to `Config { source }` when the plugin runs on a remote environment;
Go's plugin environment placement has no equivalent hook yet.

## Non-TUI core: Codex Apps tool policy (`connectors::app_tool_policy`)

Third gap found by the field-reader audit: Go parsed pieces of the Codex Apps
tool configuration but never evaluated the policy, so a tool the app
configuration disables was still offered to the model and still ran, and the
per-app/per-tool approval modes had no effect.

Evidence in the previous tree:

- `apps.AppsDefaultConfig.DestructiveEnabled` / `OpenWorldEnabled`,
  `apps.AppToolConfig.ApprovalMode` and `mcp.Tool.DestructiveHint` existed but had
  no non-test reader; `AppsConfigFromValues` parsed only `enabled` (and the
  `_default.enabled`), dropping `destructive_enabled`, `open_world_enabled`,
  `default_tools_enabled`, `default_tools_approval_mode`, `tools` and `links`.
- Go had no producer of Rust's "MCP tool call blocked by app configuration"
  outcome and no exposure-time app filter.
- Rust evaluates the policy in `connectors/src/app_tool_policy.rs`
  (`AppToolPolicyEvaluator::policy`, `app_is_enabled`,
  `apply_app_enabled_state`) and consumes it in `core/src/mcp_tool_call.rs`
  (block disabled tools, use the app approval mode), in
  `core/src/mcp_tool_exposure.rs` (`filter_codex_apps_mcp_tools`) and in
  `app-server/src/request_processors/apps_processor.rs`.

Ported:

- `apps/connectors.go` + `apps/api.go`: `AppConfig` now carries
  `approvals_reviewer`, `destructive_enabled`, `open_world_enabled`,
  `default_tools_enabled`, `default_tools_approval_mode`, `tools` and `links`;
  new `AppLinkConfig` / `AppLinksConfig`; `_default` parses destructive
  enablement, open-world enablement, the approval mode and the reviewer; an
  `appConfigFromMap` helper keeps per-app parsing in one place.
- `apps/tool_policy.go` (new): `AppToolPolicy`, `AppToolPolicyInput`,
  `AppToolPolicyEvaluator` (`Policy`, `AppEnabled`, `ApplyAppEnabledState`) and
  `AppIsEnabled`, mirroring Rust's precedence exactly: connector enablement,
  then per-tool `enabled`, then `default_tools_enabled`, then
  `(destructive_enabled || !destructive_hint) && (open_world_enabled ||
  !open_world_hint)` with both hints defaulting to true; approval precedence is
  per-tool override, then per-link default, then app default, then the apps
  default, then Auto (a tool title keys the per-tool override when the tool name
  is not found).
- `mcp/tool_approval.go`: `ToolApprovalOptions.AppPolicy`. The executor gate now
  resolves the app policy for `codex_apps` tools instead of skipping them: a
  disabled tool returns the model-visible "MCP tool call blocked by app
  configuration" output without calling the server, and an enabled tool uses the
  app approval mode in the same approval flow as custom MCP servers. Without an
  evaluator the old server-driven behavior is preserved.
- `appserver/runtime_router.go`: `filterCodexAppsRuntimeTools` ports
  `filter_codex_apps_mcp_tools` (model-visible, connector id present, policy
  enabled) and runs in `mcpRuntimeInputsForServiceWithRequirements`, so a
  disabled app tool is never exposed on any surface.
- `appserver/mcp_tool_approval.go`: builds the evaluator from the turn's
  effective config.

Tests: `apps/tool_policy_test.go` covers the config parsing surface, the
enablement precedence (`app_is_enabled` defaults and per-app overrides,
`apply_app_enabled_state`), Rust's three hint-default vectors, the approval
precedence chain, per-tool disable and `default_tools_enabled`.
`mcp/tool_approval_test.go` adds the codex_apps gate matrix (disabled tool,
disabled app, prompt mode, approve mode, unconfigured app, no evaluator).
`appserver/app_tool_policy_test.go` covers the exposure filter including the
destructive-hint policy.

Verification: `go build ./...`; `go vet ./apps ./mcp ./appserver` clean;
`gofmt -l` clean; `go test ./apps ./mcp ./tool ./turn ./appserver ./app ./parity
-count=1` green; full `go test ./... -count=1` green.

Residuals (evidence-based):

- Managed app requirements are not modeled in Go: Rust's
  `managed_app_tool_approval` (a requirement's per-tool `approval_mode`) and the
  requirements-applied `enabled = false` constraint
  (`apply_requirements_apps_constraints`) have no Go input, because Go has no
  `AppsRequirementsToml` equivalent. The evaluator's approval chain therefore
  starts at the per-tool override.
- Per-link overrides are parsed but never resolved at call time: Rust derives
  `link_id` from the account/`_codex_apps` metadata
  (`core/src/mcp_tool_call.rs::mcp_tool_metadata`), Go's executor has only the
  connector id, so `AppToolPolicyInput.LinkID` is empty on the live paths.
- The apps list/read wire path still uses `apps.WithAppEnabledState`, which
  marks every app explicit whenever an apps config exists, whereas Rust's
  `apply_app_enabled_state` only overrides configured apps; left unchanged
  because it is a listing-semantics difference, not part of this gap.

## Non-TUI core: managed app requirements (`AppsRequirementsToml`)

Closes the residual recorded with the Codex Apps tool policy: Go had no managed
`apps` requirements, so a Cloud/MDM layer could not disable an app or pin a tool
approval mode, and Rust's `managed_app_tool_approval` had no Go equivalent.

Rust reference: `config/src/config_requirements.rs` (`AppsRequirementsToml`,
`AppRequirementToml`, `AppToolsRequirementsToml`, `AppToolRequirementToml`,
`merge_app_requirements_descending`), consumed by
`connectors/src/app_tool_policy.rs` (`from_parts` -> `effective_apps_config` +
`managed_app_tool_approval`).

Ported:

- `apps/app_requirements.go` (new): the requirement types
  (`AppToolResultSourceRequirement`, `AppToolRequirement`, `AppRequirement`,
  `AppsRequirements`) with `AppsRequirementsFromMap`, `IsEmpty`,
  `MergeAppRequirementsDescending` (either layer may disable an app; an exact
  per-tool approval keeps the higher-precedence value) and
  `CloneAppsRequirements`.
- `apps/tool_policy.go`:
  `NewAppToolPolicyEvaluatorWithRequirements` ports Rust's `from_parts` -
  `effectiveAppsConfig` folds a managed `enabled = false` into the effective
  config without mutating the caller's snapshot, and `managedApproval` gives a
  required per-tool approval the highest precedence, including the explicit
  `auto` mode (Rust's `Option<AppToolApproval>` semantics, so `auto` still wins
  over a configured mode). `apply_app_enabled_state` therefore also honors
  managed disablement.
- `config/api.go`: `ConfigRequirements.Apps` (internal `json:"-"`, like
  `MCPServers`/`Plugins`) and its deep clone.
- `config/requirements_file.go`: parses the `apps` requirements table; a
  requirements document containing only `apps` is no longer treated as empty.
- `config/cloud_config.go`: `mergeConfigRequirements` merges app requirements
  descending when Cloud/MDM layers are combined.
- `appserver`: the Codex Apps evaluator is built with the thread config's
  requirements in both the executor gate and the exposure filter.

Tests: `apps/app_requirements_test.go` covers parsing, all four Rust
`merge_app_requirements_descending_*` vectors, the tool-approval precedence half
of `evaluator_reuses_one_snapshot_across_tools` (managed approve beats a
configured prompt; the tool-title alias keeps the configured mode), managed
disablement, and the no-mutation guarantee.
`config/app_requirements_test.go` covers requirements TOML parsing,
`mergeConfigRequirements` descending behavior, and the clone.
`appserver/app_tool_policy_test.go` adds the managed-requirement exposure case.

Verification: `go build ./...`; `go vet ./apps ./config ./appserver ./mcp`
clean; `gofmt -l` clean; full `go test ./... -count=1` green.

Residuals (unchanged from the previous section): per-link approval overrides are
parsed but never resolved at call time (Go's executor has no link id), and the
apps list/read wire path still uses `apps.WithAppEnabledState` rather than
`ApplyAppEnabledState`.

## Non-TUI core: managed Windows sandbox requirements (`[windows]`)

Another shape mismatch found while auditing the requirements surface: Go read the
managed Windows sandbox settings from a **top-level**
`allowed_windows_sandbox_implementations` key, while Rust's
`ConfigRequirementsToml` nests them under `[windows]`
(`WindowsRequirementsToml`). A managed `requirements.toml` written in Rust's
documented shape was therefore silently ignored by Go.

Rust evidence: `config/src/config_requirements.rs:868` (`WindowsRequirementsToml
{ allowed_sandbox_implementations, sandbox_private_desktop }`) and its own test
`deserialize_allowed_windows_sandbox_implementations` (line 3763), which parses
`[windows] allowed_sandbox_implementations = ["elevated"]` and asserts an empty
list is rejected (`empty_allowed_windows_sandbox_implementations_is_rejected`).
`requirements.windows.sandbox_private_desktop` is folded into
`config.windows.sandbox_private_desktop` by `apply_to_config`
(config_requirements.rs:1614) and exposed on the app-server wire as
`windowsSandboxPrivateDesktop` (config_processor.rs:385).

A second, related difference: Rust resolves the private-desktop knob from
`[windows] sandbox_private_desktop` with a **default of true**
(`core/src/windows_sandbox.rs::resolve_windows_sandbox_private_desktop`), while
Go read `[permissions] windows_sandbox_private_desktop` and defaulted to false,
so Windows sandboxing ran without a private desktop unless configured otherwise.

Ported:

- `config/requirements_file.go`: parses the `[windows]` requirements table
  (`allowed_sandbox_implementations` with Rust's empty-list rejection, plus
  `sandbox_private_desktop`); the legacy top-level
  `allowed_windows_sandbox_implementations` key still works and the nested table
  wins when both are present. `windowsSandboxImplementationsFromMap` holds the
  new parsing.
- `config/requirements_file.go`: `ResolveWindowsSandboxPrivateDesktop(values,
  requirements)` ports Rust's resolution chain - `[windows]
  sandbox_private_desktop` (default true), the legacy `[permissions]` shape as a
  fallback, then a managed requirement overriding both.
- `config/api.go`: `ConfigRequirements.WindowsSandboxPrivateDesktop` (internal
  field plus the `windowsSandboxPrivateDesktop` wire field Rust's
  `ConfigRequirements` exposes), with its clone.
- `config/cloud_config.go`: the layer merge carries the overlay value.
- `appserver`: `windowsSandboxPrivateDesktopForTurn(cfg)` replaces the value-only
  reader in the shell validation options and the executor skill sandbox context,
  so the managed requirement now reaches both.

Tests: `config/windows_requirements_test.go` covers the `[windows]` parse, the
empty-list rejection, the legacy key and nested precedence, the resolution chain
(default true, `[windows]` beats the legacy shape, requirement overrides either
way) and the merge/clone. `appserver`'s
`TestWindowsSandboxPrivateDesktopFromConfigValuesMatchesRust` was rewritten: it
previously pinned the wrong default (false) and the `[permissions]`-only shape.

Verification: `go build ./...`; `gofmt -l` clean; `go test ./config ./appserver
./parity ./tool -count=1` green; full `go test ./... -count=1` green.

Residual (evidence-based): Rust models the allowed implementations as a
constrained value whose *absence* is rejected and whose preferred value is
`elevated` when both implementations are allowed
(`config_requirements.rs:1915-1930`), while Go's
`ResolveAllowedWindowsSandboxSetupMode` maps `default` to `unelevated` and
returns the requested mode when the requirement list is empty. Narrowing that to
Rust's exact constraint semantics is a follow-up in the same area.

## Non-TUI core: compaction prompt config (`compact_prompt`)

Next audit pass: the `ConfigToml` field sweep (all 102 Rust top-level fields
checked for any Go reference) left only three keys with no Go reference at all -
`experimental_thread_store_endpoint`, `experimental_thread_store` and
`experimental_compact_prompt_file`. Triage:

- `experimental_compact_prompt_file` is a **supported Rust feature**: a file
  whose contents become the compaction prompt (core/src/config/mod.rs:3936-3943
  and core/src/compact.rs:123-127, `unwrap_or(SUMMARIZATION_PROMPT)`). Go rejected
  the key as unknown (it was not in the recognized-key set) and had no
  compaction-prompt config support at all - `compact_prompt` was only listed as a
  known key (`config/config.go`, and the app-server config schema) with no
  reader. Ported below.
- `experimental_thread_store` is test plumbing: Rust's `ThreadStoreToml` only has
  `Local {}` and a `#[schemars(skip)] InMemory { id }`, used by app-server tests
  (app-server/tests/suite/v2/remote_thread_store.rs and friends). Go injects its
  store directly in tests, so this stays allowlisted in the parity gate with an
  updated reason.
- `experimental_thread_store_endpoint` is **removed** in Rust and kept only to
  fail fast (core/src/config/mod.rs:3147-3152: "`experimental_thread_store_endpoint`
  is no longer supported; remove it from config.toml"). Go now fails the
  effective config load with the same message instead of ignoring the key.

Ported:

- `compact.SummarizationPrompt` (new constant in `compact/compact.go`) mirrors
  Rust `codex_prompts::SUMMARIZATION_PROMPT`
  (`codex-rs/prompts/templates/compact/prompt.md`) verbatim, so the built-in
  prompt text matches Rust instead of being absent.
- `appserver/compact_prompt.go` (new): `compactPromptForConfig` ports Rust's
  resolution - `compact_prompt` (trimmed) wins, then the
  `experimental_compact_prompt_file` contents (read on demand and required to be
  non-empty, with Rust's message shapes), then the built-in prompt.
  `compactPromptForRecord` resolves it through the thread's effective config.
- `appserver/turn_runtime.go`: `compactThreadWithHistory` fills `request.Prompt`
  from that resolution when the caller did not supply one, so auto-compaction
  (pre-turn, mid-turn, after-turn) and manual `/compact` now send the configured
  prompt to the compaction model (`agentCompactRunner` already forwarded
  `request.Prompt` as the request prompt).
- `config/config.go`: `experimental_compact_prompt_file` joins the recognized
  top-level keys, and the removed `experimental_thread_store_endpoint` fails the
  effective config load with Rust's message (next to the other always-on
  retired-setting guards).
- `parity/config_schema_surface_test.go`: the Rust-only allowlist entry for
  `experimental_compact_prompt_file` is removed (the gate requires this once Go
  recognizes the key) and the pinned Go key count moves 105 -> 106.

Tests: `appserver/compact_prompt_test.go` covers the resolution chain
(configured value, trim, blank value falling through to the file, file contents,
precedence, missing file, empty file), the record/effective-config path, and an
end-to-end `compactThread` run with a recording `CompactRunner` proving the
built-in prompt is the default and a configured `compact_prompt` reaches the
request. `config/compact_prompt_config_test.go` covers the fail-fast guard and
that the compact prompt file key is accepted (and surfaced in the loaded values).

Verification: `go build ./...`; `go vet ./compact ./config ./appserver` clean;
`gofmt -l` clean; `go test ./compact ./config ./appserver ./app ./parity -count=1`
green; full `go test ./... -count=1` green.

Residual (evidence-based): Rust's compaction request also uses
`base_instructions: sess.get_prompt_base_instructions()` (core/src/compact.rs:275),
while Go's `agentCompactRunner` still sends a hardcoded instruction block
(`remoteCompactInstructions`). Aligning that needs Go's model-catalog base
instructions resolution for a compaction thread, so it is recorded here rather
than guessed at.

## Non-TUI core: config reader sweep (second pass) and project-doc discovery

The first `ConfigToml` sweep counted any textual reference, so a key that was
only *listed* in Go's recognized-key set looked implemented - that is how
`compact_prompt` slipped past it. The sweep was rerun treating pure listing lines
(`"key": {},` and `"key",`) as non-references.

Of the 102 Rust `ConfigToml` fields, nine then had no reader:
`include_permissions_instructions`, `include_collaboration_mode_instructions`,
`mcp_oauth_credentials_store`, `project_doc_fallback_filenames`,
`background_terminal_max_timeout`, `js_repl_node_path`,
`js_repl_node_module_dirs`, `file_opener`, `hide_agent_reasoning`.

Ported this pass:

- `project_doc_fallback_filenames` and `project_root_markers`. Rust's
  `core/src/agents_md.rs::candidate_filenames` builds the candidate list
  `[AGENTS.override.md, AGENTS.md, ...configured fallbacks]` and roots discovery
  with `project_root_markers` (default `[".git"]`). Go already had the mechanics
  (`prompt.InstructionsCandidateFilenames`, `FindInstructionsProjectRoot`,
  `config.projectRootMarkersFromValues`) but `loadProjectInstructionsFor` never
  passed either value, so a configured fallback filename (for example
  `CLAUDE.md`) was never read and a custom project root marker was ignored.
  `config.ProjectDocFallbackFilenames()` / `config.ProjectRootMarkers()` are the
  new accessors and `appserver/runtime_router.go` passes both into
  `promptctx.InstructionsLoadConfig`.
- `include_collaboration_mode_instructions` (default true). Rust omits the
  collaboration-mode world-state section entirely when it is false
  (`core/src/session/world_state.rs:169`); Go always emitted it.
  `config.IncludeCollaborationModeInstructions()` gates
  `collaborationModeWorldStateInputItem`, which now takes the turn config so a
  disabled flag neither emits the item nor persists world state.

Dispositions from the same sweep:

- `js_repl_node_path` / `js_repl_node_module_dirs`: Rust deprecated ignored
  fields (schemars-skipped); Go recognizes them so strict config does not reject
  legacy files. No behavior to port - already allowlisted by the parity gate.
- `file_opener` and `hide_agent_reasoning` are TUI surfaces, so they belong to
  the TUI pass (user-directed last).
- `experimental_thread_store`: Rust's `ThreadStoreToml` only has `Local {}` and a
  schemars-skipped `InMemory { id }` used by app-server tests; Go injects its
  store directly. The parity allowlist reason was updated to record that.

Tests: `appserver/project_instructions_fallback_test.go` covers the fallback
filename being ignored by default and honored when configured, the
`AGENTS.md`-before-fallback candidate order, and the configured project root
marker driving discovery. `config/project_doc_config_test.go` and
`config/include_instructions_config_test.go` cover the accessors.
`appserver/collaboration_world_state_test.go` gains the disabled-flag case
(no item, no persisted world state) and its existing call sites now pass the
config explicitly.

Verification: `go build ./...`; `go vet ./config ./appserver` clean; `gofmt -l`
clean; `go test ./config ./appserver ./compact ./prompt ./parity -count=1` green;
full `go test ./... -count=1` green.

## Next non-TUI work item (evidence, not started): the permissions world state

`include_permissions_instructions` triaged to a real missing surface, larger than
the others, so it is recorded here rather than half-implemented:

- Rust builds a `permissions` world-state section
  (`core/src/context/world_state/permissions.rs`, section ID `permissions`) from
  `PermissionsInstructions::from_permission_profile(profile, approval_policy,
  ApprovalPromptContext, exec_policy, cwd, exec_permission_approvals,
  request_permissions_tool)`, tracking a snapshot hash (of the instructions
  without approved prefixes) plus the approved-prefix set and rendering diffs;
  when the flag is false it sends `CompactPermissionsState` (exec-policy only,
  `compact_permissions.rs`).
- Go's app server never emits that section - there is no
  `<permissions instructions>` producer outside the `codex exec` CLI path
  (`exec/exec.go:1908`). `context.PermissionsInstructions` is an orphaned legacy
  fragment with different tags (`<permissions>`), no constructor and no caller,
  and `session.WorldState.PermissionInstructions` is only ever cloned.
- `exec_policy_saved.go` already ports the `ApprovedCommandPrefixSaved` diff
  fragment, so the remaining work is the instructions builder, the section
  snapshot/diff, and the flag gate.

Also pending triage from the same sweep: `background_terminal_max_timeout`
(Rust core/src/config/mod.rs:3812; no Go reader, unified-exec background
terminal lifetime) and `mcp_oauth_credentials_store` (Rust app-server mcp
processor; Go's MCP OAuth store has no mode selection).

## Non-TUI core: permissions instructions port (stage 1 - faithful text + exec wiring)

Starting the recorded `include_permissions_instructions` item by fixing the text
and building the rendering pipeline, so the app-server world-state wiring can
hook into a Rust-faithful builder.

Current-state evidence gathered before the change:

- Go's app server never emits a `<permissions instructions>` fragment: only the
  `codex exec` CLI path did, via a hand-written inline approximation
  (`exec/exec.go` `execPermissionsInstructions`), and `sandbox.BuildPermissionPrompt`
  (a dead-code builder with no production caller) used yet another non-Rust
  wording. `context.PermissionsInstructions` is an orphaned legacy fragment with
  different markers (`<permissions>`) and no caller.
- Rust renders the fragment from `prompts/src/permissions_instructions.rs`
  (templates under `prompts/templates/permissions/`), with
  `append_section` newline semantics, `format_allow_prefixes` for approved
  prefixes, model-catalog `PermissionMessages`/`ApprovalMessages` overrides, the
  granular category lists, and the auto-review suffix.

Landed:

- `sandbox/permission_prompt_templates.go` (new): the seven Rust templates
  (`approval_policy/{never,unless_trusted,on_request,on_request_rule_request_permission}.md`
  and `sandbox_mode/{danger_full_access,workspace_write,read_only}.md`) embedded
  verbatim, with the sandbox templates trimmed of trailing whitespace exactly
  like Rust's `Template::parse(<template>.trim_end())`.
- `sandbox/permission_prompt.go`: `BuildPermissionPrompt` now mirrors Rust's
  assembly (a newline before each section, a trailing newline on the body),
  the exact `{{ network_access }}` placeholder replacement (other or malformed
  placeholders preserved verbatim, an empty catalog message dropping just the
  sandbox section), the catalog approval-message selection (including the
  on-request auto-review variant and Granular having none), the granular
  category sections plus the request-permissions rule section when
  `exec_permission_approvals` allows `sandbox_approval`, the Rust
  request-permissions tool text, the exact auto-review suffix (only when the
  policy is not `never`), Rust's writable-roots sentence, denied-reads ordering
  (roots then globs), and `FormatAllowPrefixes` for the approved-prefix section.
- `sandbox/permission_prompt_profile.go` (new):
  `BuildPermissionPromptForProfile` ports
  `PermissionsInstructions::from_permission_profile`'s mapping from a resolved
  `PermissionProfile` (full-disk write -> danger-full-access, no writable roots
  -> read-only, else workspace-write with sorted roots; network from the
  profile; denied read entries split into paths/globs and resolved against cwd)
  plus `granularPromptCategories` for Rust's category order, and the fragment
  markers with `RenderPermissionInstructions`.
- `exec/exec.go`: the `codex exec` startup input item now uses the shared
  builder (`BuildPermissionPromptForProfile` + `RenderPermissionInstructions`)
  with the turn's approval policy and the `exec_permission_approvals` /
  `request_permissions_tool` feature flags, replacing the inline approximation.

Tests: `sandbox/permission_prompt_test.go` gains Rust's
`permissions_instructions_tests.rs` vectors (the three sandbox-mode texts, the
catalog message selection, the missing/empty/invalid placeholder cases,
network-access rendering) plus exact-body assertions for the never policy and
the approval/catalog selection matrix including the auto-review suffix rules;
`exec_test.go` updated for the new signature.

Verification: `go build ./...`; `gofmt -l` clean for the touched files (the
`sandbox/windowssandbox` CRLF files gofmt flags are pre-existing);
`go test ./sandbox ./exec ./tool ./appserver ./parity -count=1` green.

## Next stage for the same item (design recorded, not started)

App-server world-state section, mirroring Rust
`core/src/context/world_state/permissions.rs`:

- Section ID `permissions` with snapshot `{ hash(instructions without approved
  prefixes), approved prefix set }`: re-emit the full fragment when the
  instructions hash changes, and emit the `ApprovedCommandPrefixSaved` fragment

- Needs: `config.IncludePermissionsInstructions()`; a granular
  `GranularApprovalConfig` accessor for the turn; an allowed-prefix accessor over

  currently pass as nil).

## Non-TUI core: permissions world state (stage 2 - app-server section and gate)

Completes the `include_permissions_instructions` item's wiring.

- `sandbox/permission_prompt_profile.go`: `WorldStateFragmentHash` ports Rust
  `WorldStateHash::from_fragment` (SHA-1 over
  `codex-world-state-fragment-v1\0` plus the length-prefixed role and rendered
  fragment, CRLF-normalized), so the persisted section snapshot is
  hash-compatible with Rust.
- `config`: `IncludePermissionsInstructions()` (default true).
- `appserver/permissions_world_state.go` (new):
  - `permissionPromptOptionsForTurn` assembles the turn's approval policy,
    approvals reviewer, granular categories (from the config or turn
    overrides, in Rust's category order) and the `exec_permission_approvals` /
    `request_permissions_tool` feature flags.
  - `permissionsInstructionsForTurn` renders the fragment from the resolved
    permission profile and computes the section hash from the instructions built
    *without* approved prefixes, exactly like Rust (so prefix-only changes keep
    the hash stable).
  - `permissionsWorldStateInputItem` mirrors
    `PermissionsState::render_diff`: the fragment is emitted when the section was
    absent, unknown, or its instructions hash changed, and the snapshot
    (`{"instructions": <sha1>, "approved_command_prefixes": [...]}`) is persisted
    on `session.WorldState.PermissionInstructions` (the field already existed but
    was never populated).
  - `permissionsWorldStateSessionItemForTurn` keeps the emitted fragment in the
    thread history so later requests retain it, mirroring the collaboration-mode
    section's handling.
- `appserver/turn_runtime.go`: the section is assembled immediately before the
  collaboration-mode section (Rust's world-state order) and its session item is
  persisted alongside the realtime/collaboration ones.

Residual recorded for the next stage: the approved-prefix half of the diff. Rust
emits `ApprovedCommandPrefixSaved` from this section when the prefix set grows
and sends `CompactPermissionsState` (section id `approved_command_prefixes`)
when the flag is off; Go still reports saved prefixes through the existing
turn-scoped `execPolicyPostToolInputItems` path, and
`permissionPromptOptionsForTurn` therefore leaves `ApprovedCommandPrefixes`
empty until an allowed-prefix accessor over `execpolicy.Policy` (plus the
thread's session-saved prefixes) lands. Model-catalog `permissions` /
`approvals` message overrides also remain unparsed in Go (`model.ModelMessages`
has no equivalents), so those inputs pass as nil.

Tests: `appserver/permissions_world_state_test.go` pins the hash against an
independently computed SHA-1 vector (including CRLF normalization), the
absent -> emit + persist lifecycle, suppression when the instructions hash is
unchanged, re-emission after an approval-policy change, the session-item shape,
and the disabled-flag gate; `config/include_instructions_config_test.go` covers
the new accessor.

Verification: `go build ./...`; `go vet ./appserver` clean; `gofmt -l` clean for
the touched files; `go test ./config ./sandbox ./exec ./appserver ./parity
-count=1` green; full `go test ./... -count=1` green.

## Non-TUI core: permissions world state (stage 3 - approved-prefix diff)

Completes the permissions section by porting the approved-prefix half and
removing the earlier approximation.

- `execpolicy/execpolicy.go`: `Policy.AllowedPrefixes` ports Rust
  `Policy::get_allowed_prefixes` (every `Decision::Allow` rule's pattern, with
  per-token alternatives rendered as `[a|b]` per Rust `render_pattern_token`),
  plus `CanonicalCommandPrefixes` for deterministic set comparison/persistence.
- `appserver/exec_policy_saved.go`: the turn-scoped fragment queue was replaced
  by a per-thread deduped approved-prefix set (Rust's session exec policy
  accumulates amendments); `approvedCommandPrefixesForThread` combines the
  on-disk policy's allow prefixes (`loadedExecPolicy`, tolerating a missing
  rules file) with the session-approved ones. The former
  `execPolicyPostToolInputItems` injection and its turn-runtime wiring are gone,
  because Rust reports saved prefixes only through the world-state diff.
- `appserver/permissions_world_state.go`: the full section now stores the
  approved prefix set in its snapshot and mirrors `PermissionsState::render_diff`
  exactly - an unchanged instructions hash with a grew-by-subset prefix set
  emits the `ApprovedCommandPrefixSaved` fragment for the added prefixes, a
  removed prefix falls back to re-rendering the full instructions, and an
  absent/unknown section renders the instructions. `compactPermissionsWorldStateUpdate`
  ports `CompactPermissionsState` (section id `approved_command_prefixes`) for
  `include_permissions_instructions = false`: an unknown/absent previous state
  emits nothing and only newly added prefixes are reported.
- `session/world_state.go`: `ApprovedCommandPrefixes` carries the compact
  section snapshot (the field was missing).

Tests: `appserver/exec_policy_saved_test.go` now covers the world-state route
(full instructions first, one saved-prefix report after an approved amendment,
nothing on repeat) and the per-thread set; `appserver/permissions_world_state_test.go`
adds the compact-mode behavior (nothing before a previous snapshot, one report
after an amendment) and asserts the compact snapshot is what gets persisted
while the permissions instructions section is not.

Verification: `go build ./...`; `go vet ./appserver ./execpolicy ./session`
clean; `gofmt -l` clean for the touched files; full `go test ./... -count=1` green
apart from the pre-existing load-sensitive
`TestRuntimeRouterMCPEventStreamStartStopForwardsAndScopesLikeRust` flake, which
passes in isolation.

Remaining for this item: model-catalog `permissions` / `approvals` message
overrides are still unparsed in Go (`model.ModelMessages` has no equivalents), so
`PermissionMessages`/`ApprovalMessages` pass as nil.
