## Non-TUI core: background terminal max timeout

The last unread key from the config reader sweep with a real behavior behind it.
Rust wires `background_terminal_max_timeout` into
`UnifiedExecProcessManager::new(...)` (core/src/session/session.rs:1536), where
it becomes `max_write_stdin_yield_time_ms`: the cap on how long a unified-exec
call yields to a background terminal, floored at `MIN_EMPTY_YIELD_TIME_MS`
(5_000) and defaulting to `DEFAULT_MAX_BACKGROUND_TERMINAL_TIMEOUT_MS`
(300_000) - the same value Go already ships in `config/defaults.toml` but never
read.

Ported:

- `config`: `DefaultBackgroundTerminalMaxTimeoutMS` (300_000) and
  `BackgroundTerminalMaxTimeoutMS()` (Rust's `unwrap_or(default)`), accepting the
  integer shapes the config layer produces and falling back to the default for
  unset, zero, negative or mistyped values. The minimum-yield clamp stays with
  the caller, matching where Rust applies it.
- `tool`: `UnifiedExecManager.SetMaxEmptyPollYieldTime` (clamped to
  `unifiedExecMinEmptyPollYieldMS`) and the field is now an atomic so the
  request-time reader in `clampWriteYield` stays race-free.
- `appserver/runtime_router.go`: the turn-scoped tool router applies the turn
  config's value to the shared manager before handing it to the registry.
- `exec/exec.go`: the exec runner applies the run config's value before building
  the tool registry.

Tests: `config/background_terminal_timeout_test.go` covers the accessor's
default/valid/invalid shapes; `tool/unified_exec_yield_test.go` covers the clamp
(empty-poll yields are capped by the setting and floored at the minimum, non-empty
writes keep their own cap, nil manager is a no-op).

Verification: `go build ./...`; `go vet ./config ./tool ./appserver` clean;
`gofmt -l` clean for the touched files; full `go test ./... -count=1` green.

Remaining sweep item: `mcp_oauth_credentials_store` (Rust app-server MCP
processor / plugins path; Go's MCP OAuth store has no mode selection).

## Next non-TUI item (triage recorded): `mcp_oauth_credentials_store`

Last unread key from the config reader sweep. Evidence and shape:

- Rust resolves `mcp_oauth_credentials_store` into
  `config.mcp_oauth_credentials_store_mode`
  (`core/src/config/mod.rs:281` `resolve_mcp_oauth_credentials_store_mode`,
  field at `:860`) and passes it - together with
  `auth_keyring_backend_kind` - into MCP OAuth login and token storage:
  `app-server/src/request_processors/mcp_processor.rs:222` and
  `plugins.rs:1854`, plus `core/src/mcp_skill_dependencies.rs:198/218`. The mode
  selects `file`, `keyring` or `auto` credential storage.
- Go's MCP OAuth store is file-only: `mcp.NewOAuthStore(codexHome)` wraps a
  private fallback file (`mcp/oauth.go` `OAuthStore`, `readFallbackFile` /
  `oauthFallbackEntry`), and it is constructed in
  `mcp/api.go:697`, `:1822`, `:2421`, `mcp/enterprise_oauth_login.go:165`,
  `mcp/http_client.go:53`, `:1306`, `:1338`. Nothing reads the config key, and
  Go's keyring support (`auth.Store` with `Load`/`Save`/`Delete` backends,
  `auth.go:103-105`, `NewStoreWithOptions`) is scoped to `AuthDotJSON` rather
  than per-MCP-server tokens.
- Implementation outline for the next pass: resolve the mode from the effective
  config (default mirroring Rust's `auto`), add a mode-aware token store that
  uses the OS keyring for `keyring`/`auto` with the existing fallback file as the
  `auto`/unavailable path, and thread the resolved mode into the
  `NewOAuthStore` call sites (the MCP runtime config already carries
  `CodexHome`, so the mode can travel the same way).

This is the last recorded non-TUI gap; the remaining work after it is the TUI
pass (user-directed last).

## Non-TUI core: MCP OAuth credential store mode

Closes the last recorded sweep item. Rust resolves `mcp_oauth_credentials_store`
into `config.mcp_oauth_credentials_store_mode`
(`core/src/config/mod.rs:281`, field at `:860`) and threads it through MCP OAuth
login, refresh and status (`app-server/.../mcp_processor.rs:222`,
`plugins.rs:1854`, `core/src/mcp_skill_dependencies.rs:198/218`). The enum
(`config/src/types.rs:126`) is `Auto` (default: prefer the keyring, fall back to
the credentials file when keyring storage is unavailable), `File`
(`CODEX_HOME/.credentials.json`) and `Keyring` ("keyring when available,
otherwise fail").

Go's MCP OAuth store was file-only and never read the key. Ported:

- `mcp/oauth_credentials_store.go` (new): the mode type and parser, the keyring
  availability fact and the keyring-unavailable error. Go does not link an OS
  keyring (the `auth` package's `KeyringStore` is an in-process emulation), so
  the keyring is never a durable credential source here: `auto` therefore
  resolves to the credentials file - which is exactly Rust's documented fallback
  when keyring storage is unavailable - and `keyring` fails with a clear message
  instead of silently losing credentials, matching Rust's "otherwise fail".
- `mcp/oauth.go`: `OAuthStore` carries the mode, `NewOAuthStoreWithMode` builds
  it (an unrecognized mode normalizes to auto), `effectiveMode` resolves auto
  from the platform's fixed keyring availability (Rust resolves the store once
  per client lifetime), and `Load`/`Save`/`Delete` fail early for an unusable
  keyring mode via `keyringUnavailable`. `NewOAuthStore` keeps auto.
- `mcp/config.go`: `RuntimeConfig.OAuthCredentialsStoreMode` parsed from the
  values map (`RuntimeConfigFromValues...`) and copied onto every server config
  (like the existing global `mcp_oauth_callback_*` defaults), so the ad-hoc
  credential-refresh sites can honor it.
- `mcp/api.go`, `mcp/http_client.go`, `mcp/enterprise_oauth_login.go`,
  `mcp/enterprise_oauth_flow.go`: every MCP OAuth store construction now uses the
  configured mode (the enterprise guard/delete/commit paths take it as an
  argument and `EnterpriseOAuthLoginOptions` carries it).
- `config`: `ValidateMCPOAuthCredentialsStoreMode` (Rust's enum parse fails the
  load), `MCPOAuthCredentialsStoreMode()` and the always-on load check next to
  the other retired-setting guards. Values are trimmed/lowercased following the
  Go config layer's convention, so a case variant is accepted where Rust's serde
  match would reject it; an unrecognized value still fails.

Tests: `mcp/oauth_credentials_store_test.go` covers the parser, the mode
resolution (auto -> file, file, keyring -> error, invalid -> auto), the
save/load round trip in file/auto modes and the keyring failure, and the value
flowing into the runtime and each server config.
`config/mcp_oauth_credentials_store_test.go` covers validation, the accessor and
the load rejection for an invalid value. Existing enterprise OAuth tests were
updated for the new mode arguments.

Verification: `go build ./...`; `go vet ./mcp ./config` clean; `gofmt -l` clean
for the touched files; full `go test ./... -count=1` green.

With this, every key the config reader sweep flagged has a reader; the remaining
work in the objective is the TUI pass (user-directed last).

## Non-TUI core: keyring credential storage contract (silent credential loss)

Found while auditing whole-crate coverage: Rust's `codex-secrets` crate provides
the encrypted local secrets storage behind the `Secrets` keyring backend
(`login/src/auth/storage.rs` uses `SecretsManager` / `LocalSecretsNamespace` /
`SecretName`), while Go's `auth` keyring backend is an in-process map
(`auth/keyring.go` `KeyringStore`, `defaultKeyringValues`) with no OS keyring
integration anywhere in the tree (no keyring dependency in `go.mod`).

The concrete defect: `autoAuthStorage.Save` wrote to the in-process keyring,
which always succeeds, and returned without ever writing `auth.json` - so with
`cli_auth_credentials_store = "auto"` (and the `secrets` backend enabled, which
Go defaults to on Windows when `features.secret_auth_storage` is unset)
credentials were silently kept in memory and lost when the process exited.
`keyringAuthStorage` additionally deleted the credentials file after such a
write.

Ported this pass (Rust's documented contract, which is what a host without a
keyring must observe):

- `auth/keyring.go`: `OSKeyringAvailable` (false in this build) and
  `KeyringUnavailableError`.
- `auth/auth.go`: `keyringAuthStorage` knows whether its store is durable (an
  explicitly injected store is the test seam; this build has no OS keyring) and
  `Load`/`Save`/`Delete` fail with the unavailable error otherwise. `auto` then
  takes its documented fallback to the credentials file - `autoAuthStorage`
  already falls back on a keyring error - so credentials persist again, while
  `keyring` fails loudly like Rust instead of silently using memory.

Tests: `auth/auth_test.go` covers the keyring-mode failure (save/load/delete)
and that auto mode persists to `auth.json` and reads it back. Three integration
tests that had pinned the memory-only behavior were updated to the aligned
contract: `app`'s login test now asserts the keyring-mode failure plus the auto
fallback persisting, `appserver`'s router test asserts the auth status surfaces
the unreadable store, and `doctor`'s auth check asserts the
"stored credentials could not be read" failure carrying the unavailable-keyring
detail. The injected-store keyring unit test still passes unchanged.

## Remaining non-TUI prerequisite (evidence): OS keyring backend

Two surfaces cannot be ported faithfully until Go has a durable keyring:

- `codex-secrets` encrypted local secrets storage: age **scrypt
  passphrase**-encrypted files (`local.age`, `codex_auth.age`,
  `mcp_oauth.age`; `secrets/src/local.rs:40-56`) whose passphrase lives in the
  keyring (`compute_keyring_account` / `keyring_service`). Without a durable
  keyring the passphrase would not survive a restart, so the encrypted file
  would be unreadable - the missing piece is the keyring backend, not age
  (`filippo.io/age` provides the compatible Go implementation).
- The MCP OAuth `keyring` mode already fails loudly for the same reason (see the
  mcp_oauth_credentials_store note above).

Also recorded (approximation, not a missing RPC): Go implements `fs/watch` with
a 100 ms poller (`appserver/fs.go`, `ConnectionFileWatchManager`) instead of
Rust's `file-watcher` crate on top of OS change notifications, so change
delivery latency and coalescing differ even though the observable "did these
paths change" contract holds.

## TUI pass: MCP-elicitation transport for tool approvals

First TUI-pass item, and it closes the residual recorded with the custom MCP tool
approval policy: Go surfaced that approval through `tool/requestUserInput` even
though Rust prefers the MCP elicitation transport while the
`tool_call_mcp_elicitation` feature is on (stable, default enabled in Go).

Go's TUI already had the consumer half: `tui/bottom_pane/mcp_server_elicitation.go`
detects an approval form from `meta["response_mode"] == "approval_action"` or a
single select field carrying the `accept`/`accept_session`/`accept_always`/
`decline`/`cancel` values, renders the approval options, and returns
`{action, _meta: {persist: "session"|"always"}}` (`tui/tea/modal.go` ->
`app/remote_tui.go`), and the app-server request path forwards both the schema
and the meta to the TUI.

Ported:

- `mcp/tool_approval.go`: `ParseMCPToolApprovalElicitationResponse` ports Rust
  `parse_mcp_tool_approval_elicitation_response` (an accepted elicitation uses
  `_meta.persist` = session/always, otherwise its content is read as a
  user-input answer with an unanswered accept counting as approved; a decline is
  a *rejection* and anything else aborts), plus
  `MCPToolApprovalRejectedMessage` ("user rejected MCP tool call") and the
  `MCPToolApprovalReject` decision the executor now reports with that text -
  distinct from the abort message, like Rust.
- `appserver/mcp_tool_approval.go`: `approveViaElicitation` sends the approval
  through the MCP elicitation broker when the feature is on
  (`ServerRequestMCPElicitation` with Rust's `codex_request_type` /
  `codex_approval_kind` / `tool_name` / `tool_params` / `tool_title` /
  `tool_description` / `connector_id` meta and the `persist` capability), with a
  single `decision` select field whose enum values are the TUI's approval values
  and whose labels are Rust's option labels; `response_mode: approval_action`
  makes the detection explicit. The user-input path remains the fallback when
  the feature is off, exactly like Rust.

Tests: `mcp/tool_approval_test.go` covers the elicitation answer mapping
(persist session/always, content answers, decline -> reject, cancel/unknown ->
abort) and the rejection text at the executor gate;
`appserver/mcp_tool_approval_test.go` drives the transport end to end through a
server-request sink, asserting the request id, meta, approval option values and
the resulting session-remembered decision, plus that a decline rejects without
remembering.

Verification: `go build ./...`; `go vet ./mcp ./appserver` clean; `gofmt -l`
clean for the touched files; full `go test ./... -count=1` green.

Remaining TUI-pass items: `file_opener` and `hide_agent_reasoning`, the
non-Windows OSC 10/11 shimmer palette source, the user-verification TUI flow
(blocked on the `openai/userVerification` elicitation producer and a native
provider), and the other recorded TUI residuals.

## Non-TUI core and TUI surface: agent reasoning in exec output

Re-triaged from the sweep list: `hide_agent_reasoning` is not a TUI setting -
Rust consumes it in `exec/src/event_processor_with_human_output.rs:57`
(`show_agent_reasoning: !config.hide_agent_reasoning`) to decide whether
`codex exec` renders reasoning items to stderr.

Go never rendered reasoning items in its human output, and its exec item builder
kept only the summary text, so the protocol item's `content` (raw reasoning) was
dropped as well.

Ported:

- `config`: `HideAgentReasoning()` (default false, mirroring Rust's
  `unwrap_or(false)` and `defaults.toml`).
- `protocol.ThreadItem` gains `summary` and `content` (the latter from
  `reasoningContent`/`content`), matching Rust's
  `ThreadItem::Reasoning { summary, content }` wire shape that Go's app server
  already serializes. `exec`'s reasoning item builder now fills both instead of
  flattening to `Text`, and an item with only raw content is emitted rather than
  dropped (Rust's item conversion is unconditional), which updates
  `TestEmitFinalEventsDropsReasoningWithoutSummary` to
  `TestEmitFinalEventsKeepsReasoningWithRawContentLikeRust`.
- `exec/human_render.go`: `reasoningText` ports Rust's `reasoning_text` (raw
  content wins when `show_raw_agent_reasoning` is on and non-empty, else the
  summary), and `renderItemCompleted` prints it dimmed on the reasoning item
  completion, skipped when empty or when `hide_agent_reasoning` is set. The
  renderer takes the two flags (defaults keep the previous behavior for callers
  that pass none) and the exec runner resolves them from the run config.

Tests: `exec/exec_test.go` covers the `reasoning_text` vectors, the renderer's
hide gate plus summary/raw/empty rendering, and the kept raw-content item;
`config/hide_agent_reasoning_test.go` covers the accessor.

Verification: `go build ./...`; `go vet ./exec ./protocol ./config` clean apart
from the pre-existing `exec/agent_controller.go` copylocks warning; `gofmt -l`
clean for the touched files; full `go test ./... -count=1` green.

Remaining TUI-pass items: `file_opener`, the non-Windows OSC 10/11 shimmer
palette source, the user-verification TUI flow (blocked on the
`openai/userVerification` elicitation producer and a native provider), and the
other recorded TUI residuals.

## Non-TUI core: faithful model-catalog cache identity (#43897/#43906)

Replaces the earlier sync31 approximation (`a47bd50`, provider id + account
type/plan/email + credential hash) with a port of Rust
`model-provider/src/models_identity.rs::identity`.

- `model/models_identity.go` (new): `ModelsCatalogIdentity` builds the
  `models-cache-v1` digest exactly like Rust - length-prefixed fields in Rust's
  order, with the routed provider name/base URL/sorted query params, the
  capability booleans (`requires_openai_auth`, command auth), the precise auth
  mode, the account id/user id/email/plan/raw plan/FedRAMP claims, and the
  resolved header map. Provider routing now flows through the shared
  `ProviderInfo.ToAPIProvider` (effective backend auth mode; externally managed
  ChatGPT tokens normalize to `chatgpt`), so the default base URL and the
  provider/env header map are the same ones the endpoint actually uses.
- ChatGPT access tokens stay out of the digest, so a token refresh keeps the
  catalog for the same account/user/plan; the resolved auth headers are folded
  in only for non-stable owners (API keys, command auth) or an explicit provider
  bearer credential. An unavailable credential (`api_key()?`) or an invalid
  provider config yields an empty identity, which disables cache reuse.
- `model/models_endpoint.go`: the older `ModelsCatalogIdentity(providerID, ...)`
  helper (provider id + credential SHA-256) is removed.
- `model/provider.go`, `appserver/runtime_router.go`: both managers pass the
  provider info plus the resolved auth headers.
- `auth/account.go`: `ChatGPTPlanTypeRawFromAuth` (Rust
  `get_chatgpt_plan_type_raw`) - the unparsed plan string from the token data, so
  two distinct unknown plans scope to different catalogs.

Tests: `model/models_identity_test.go` mirrors the three Rust
`models_identity_tests` vectors - token refresh keeps the key while email, user
id, account id, plan, auth mode, and missing auth change it; provider base URL,
explicit bearer token, provider headers and env credentials change it; and
`requires_openai_auth` plus two unknown raw plans differ. Also covered: a
missing env-key credential yields no identity, and the raw ChatGPT access token
never leaks into the digest.

Verification: `go build ./...`; `gofmt -l` clean for the touched files;
`go test ./model/... ./auth/... -count=1` and `go test ./appserver -count=1`
green (the only vet noise is the pre-existing `responses_agent.go` copylocks
warning).

Residual: Rust's `enforce_managed_residency` also folds the managed residency
header into the identity provider; Go applies residency at request time
(`ResponsesAgentRunner.Residency`) rather than in the provider header map, so a
residency change alone does not churn the catalog identity. The digest bytes are
an implementation detail (Go and Rust frame optional values differently); only
the scoping rules match.

## Non-TUI core: strict-config MCP server field allowlist (Rust `RawMcpServerConfig`)

Auditing the new task-tools MCP transport surfaced a stale allowlist: Go's
`config.knownStrictMCPServerFields` (used by the strict config load, mirroring
Rust's `#[schemars(deny_unknown_fields)]`) rejected every `mcp_servers.<name>`
field that Go's own MCP config parser reads but the list never named -
`default_tools_approval_mode`, `tools`, `enabled_tools`, `disabled_tools`,
`omit_tools_from`, `supports_parallel_tool_calls`, `startup_timeout_sec`,
`startup_timeout_ms`, `tool_timeout_sec`, `http_headers_helper`,
`bearer_token`, and the legacy `name`. Rust's `RawMcpServerConfig`
(`config/src/mcp_types.rs`) accepts all of them, so a valid Rust config failed
Go's strict load.

- `config/config.go`: the allowlist now mirrors Rust's field set (stdio,
  streamable HTTP, and shared fields, grouped by comment). The legacy Go-only
  spellings (`oauth_client_id`/`oauth_callback_port`/`oauth_callback_url`,
  `disabled_reason`) are retained; an unknown field still fails.

Test: `config/config_test.go::TestStrictConfigAcceptsRustMCPServerFields` loads
the full Rust field set (both transports, the shared fields, and a nested
`tools.<name>` entry with `approval_mode` + `output_token_limit`) and asserts an
unknown field is still rejected.

Verification: `go build ./...`; `go vet ./config ./app` clean; `go test ./config
./app ./parity -count=1` green.

Residual: Go still does not validate the nested `tools.<name>` / `env_vars`
entry shapes the way Rust's `McpServerToolConfig` / `McpServerEnvVar`
deny-unknown-fields, so strict mode is more permissive there.

## TUI pass: local task-tools MCP transport (`dynamic_tools_mcp.rs`)

Closes the recorded residual that Go served the `codex_tui` task-management
namespace only through the app-server dynamic-tools callback transport. Rust
prefers a local streamable-HTTP MCP server for a daemon-connected TUI
(`DynamicToolMcpServer` + `ThreadToolTransport::Mcp`), which is what gives the
task-mutation tools their approval gating (`default_tools_approval_mode:
approve`, `prompt` for `create_thread`/`send_message_to_thread`/`fork_thread`).

- `app/task_tools_mcp.go` (new): the hosted server - a loopback listener at
  `/mcp` with a random bearer token, the Rust `server_config` (url, the
  `Authorization` header, the approval modes, and the per-tool overrides),
  JSON-RPC `initialize`/`tools/list`/`tools/call`/`ping` handling, the
  read-only annotations for the query tools, the `_meta`/`x-codex-turn-metadata`
  task-field lookup, and the routing into `ExecuteDynamicTool`. Invalid or
  non-JSON bodies return JSON-RPC errors, notifications are acknowledged with
  202, and an unknown method returns `-32601`.
- `app/task_tools_mcp.go`: `taskToolsMCPHost` keeps one server alive for the
  TUI session and swaps the live connection per turn (Rust
  `DynamicToolMcpServer::reconnect`/`suspend`), so the listener URL stays stable
  across Go's per-turn app-server clients and a suspended connection fails an
  in-flight call with Rust's `TUI is reconnecting; tool was not sent`.
- `app/remote_tui.go`: a local-daemon session starts the host
  (`interactiveRemoteEndpointIsLocal`), `thread/start` drops `dynamicTools` and
  carries the `mcp_servers.codex_tui` override instead
  (`applyTaskToolTransport`, Rust `ThreadToolTransport::configure`), `thread/fork`
  params for side conversations and the safety-buffering retry merge the same
  override (`ThreadToolTransport::configure_mcp`), and the per-turn client
  attaches itself to the host after `initialize` and suspends it on exit. The
  remote-workspace case keeps the callback transport.

Tests: `app/task_tools_mcp_test.go` drives the hosted server over HTTP
(negotiated protocol version, the full namespace with the read-only hints, and
the reconnecting failure after `suspend`), and an end-to-end `thread/start`
against a scripted app server asserting that `dynamicTools` is gone while the
`mcp_servers.codex_tui` override points at the host's listener and the thread is
reported as task-tools-capable.

Verification: `go build ./...`; `go vet ./app` clean; `gofmt -l` clean;
`go test ./app ./config ./parity ./tui/... -count=1` green.

Residuals: the prompt/compact/edit-fresh-thread paths and the dashboard dispatch
client still build their own `remoteThreadStartParams` and therefore use the
callback transport; the hosted server implements the legacy `initialize`
handshake (the client falls back from `server/discover`), and the `Mcp-Session-Id`
header is not issued.

## Non-TUI core: per-server parallel tool-call opt-in (`supports_parallel_tool_calls`)

Found while closing the strict-config allowlist: Go accepted (or, before the
allowlist fix, rejected) `mcp_servers.<name>.supports_parallel_tool_calls` but
never honored it. Rust couples `McpServerConfig::supports_parallel_tool_calls`
to `McpServerMetadata::supports_parallel_tool_calls`, copies it onto every
`ToolInfo`, and the tool router gates each call's parallelism on it
(`core/src/tools/router.rs`, `tools/handlers/mcp.rs`). Go's tool dispatcher has
the same per-tool gate (`Router.SupportsParallel` -> `spec.Parallel`), but the
MCP executor's `Parallel` was only `readOnlyHint`, so a server that opts in
could never make its mutating tools parallel-safe.

- `mcp/config.go`: `ServerConfig.SupportsParallelToolCalls` (wire
  `supports_parallel_tool_calls`) parsed via the new `runtimeConfigBoolAny`
  (snake_case or camelCase; a mistyped value is ignored like the rest of the
  typed config layer).
- `turn/tools.go`: `mcpServerParallelToolCalls` resolves the opt-in per server
  from the effective runtime config (the same lookup the `omit_tools_from`
  policy uses), and `registerMCPToolSet` passes it as the executor's `Parallel`;
  the read-only-hint rule still applies on top.

Tests: `mcp/config_approval_test.go::TestRuntimeServerConfigFromValuesSupportsParallelToolCalls`
covers both spellings, explicit false, absent, and a mistyped value;
`turn/tools_parallel_test.go::TestMCPToolParallelOptInFollowsServerConfigLikeRust`
builds a registry with one opted-in and one plain server and asserts the router
gate (`true` for the opt-in server's mutating tool, `false` for the other).

Verification: `go build ./...`; `go vet ./mcp ./turn ./config` clean; `gofmt -l`
clean; `go test ./mcp ./turn ./config -count=1` green.

### Task-tools MCP transport: end-to-end verification added

The MCP transport landed last pass was verified only against a scripted app
server. Two integration tests now prove it against the real MCP runtime and a
full tool call:

- `TestTaskToolsMCPServerServesTheMCPRuntimeLikeRust` builds a `mcp.MCPService`
  from the thread's `mcp_servers.codex_tui` override (the same
  `ServerConfigFromValues` path the app server uses), lets the real MCP HTTP
  client initialize the hosted server and run `tools/list`, and asserts the whole
  namespace is discovered plus the resolved `approve`-default / `prompt`-for-
  `create_thread` approval policy.
- `TestTaskToolsMCPToolCallRoutesThroughTheAppServerLikeRust` sends an MCP
  `tools/call` for `list_threads` and asserts it runs against the TUI's
  app-server connection (the scripted server answers `thread/list`) and returns
  the payload as text content.

Verification: `go build ./...`; `go vet ./app` clean; `gofmt -l` clean;
`go test ./app -count=1` green.

## Non-TUI core: retire the `remote_compaction_v2` toggle like Rust

A registry-by-registry diff of Rust's `FEATURES` table
(`features/src/lib.rs`) against Go's `features.Registry`
(143 entries each, stage + default) found exactly one divergence:
`remote_compaction_v2` was `Stage::Removed`/disabled in Rust but
`StageStable`/default-on in Go, with a bespoke header exception.

Rust (`apply_map`) explicitly *skips* the key - a `features.remote_compaction_v2`
entry is recorded but never applied - and
`Session::build_model_client_beta_features_header` advertises the key
unconditionally (`spec.id == Feature::RemoteCompactionV2 || ...`). Go instead
let an explicit `features.remote_compaction_v2 = false` drop the key from the
model-request beta header, and reported it as a stable, default-on feature.

- `features/features.go`: the registry entry is now `StageRemoved` (default
  off), with an explanatory comment; `remoteCompactionV2Key` names the key; and
  `ModelClientBetaFeaturesHeader` advertises `remote_compaction_v2`
  unconditionally while the remaining entries stay
  "experimental stage + enabled". The bespoke
  `featureAdvertisedInModelClientHeader` predicate is gone.
- `features/legacy.go`: `ignoredFeatureKeys` now also covers
  `remote_compaction_v2` (Rust's `apply_map` skip) and `item_ids` (Rust skips it
  in the `image_detail_original | resize_all_images | item_ids` arm and Go was
  missing it).

Tests: `features/features_test.go` pins the unconditional header (including an
explicit disable), the enabled-experiment ordering, the retired registry spec,
and that `ResolveSettings` ignores the two retired toggles while still applying
a live one.

Verification: `go build ./...`; `gofmt -l` clean; `go test ./features ./config
./model ./app ./parity -count=1` and `go test ./appserver -count=1` green.

## Parity gate: feature-registry stage/default drift check

The manual registry diff that found the `remote_compaction_v2` divergence is now
a standing L0 check, so the next drift fails the build instead of waiting for an
audit.

- `parity/rust_features_registry_test.go` (new):
  `TestFeaturesRegistryMatchesRust` parses Rust's `FEATURES` table
  (`features/src/lib.rs`) and compares every `FeatureSpec` against Go's
  `features.Registry` by stage and default enablement - resolving the
  `cfg!(any(target_os = ...))` stage (`prevent_idle_sleep`) and the
  `cfg!(windows)` default (`secret_auth_storage`) for the running host - and
  additionally rejects a Go key Rust does not declare and a missing Rust key.
  It pins the full 143-entry surface, not just the key set that
  `TestRustFeatureKeySurfaceAgainstGo` already covered.
- `parity/contracts/manifest.json`: the `features` contract's verifier list gains
  the new test alongside `TestRustFeatureKeySurfaceAgainstGo`.

Verification: `gofmt -l` clean; `go vet ./parity` clean; `go test ./parity
-count=1` green (the new check fails on the pre-fix registry, which is how the
`remote_compaction_v2` gap was found).

## Non-TUI core: model-catalog `approvals` / `persistent_instructions` (wire-name fix)

A field-by-field diff of Go's `model.ModelMessages` against Rust
`protocol::openai_models::ModelMessages` (and the real
`models-manager/models.json`) found the catalog keys Go parses do not match the
catalog keys Rust ships:

- Go read `json:"approval"` for `Approvals`; the catalog serializes
  **`approvals`** (`"approvals": {"on_request": ..., "on_request_auto_review":
  ...}` in `models.json`). Every catalog-provided approval-message override was
  therefore silently dropped, so the permissions/approval prompt texts the
  model declares never replaced the built-ins.
- The catalog also ships **`persistent_instructions`**, which Go never parsed:
  `appserver/turn_runtime.go` read a `record.Metadata.Extra` key that no code
  ever writes, so the persistent-mode developer fragment always used the
  bundled default.
- The bundled default itself was an approximation; Rust's is
  `core/assets/persistent_mode.md` (a longer document that also mentions
  `functions.send_user_message_async` in its prose).

Changes:

- `model/catalog.go`: `Approvals` is `json:"approvals"` in both the struct tag
  and the custom `UnmarshalJSON`, and `ModelMessages` gains
  `PersistentInstructions *string json:"persistent_instructions"` (pointer so a
  missing value stays distinct from the explicit-empty disable).
- `context/templates/persistent_mode.md` (new, vendored verbatim from Rust,
  LF-normalized) plus `context/persistent_mode.go`: the bundled default is now
  the embedded Rust asset, and `PersistentModeInstructions` takes
  `catalogInstructions *string` so `nil` uses the default while an explicit `""`
  disables the section exactly like Rust
  (`catalog_instructions.unwrap_or(DEFAULT_INSTRUCTIONS).trim()`).
- `appserver/turn_runtime.go`: the persistent-mode fragment passes the model
  catalog's `PersistentInstructions` (the dead record-metadata read is gone).

Tests: `model/catalog_messages_test.go` pins the catalog wire names (all four
`approvals` fields, `persistent_instructions`, and that the legacy singular
`approval` key must *not* populate the field) and asserts the bundled Rust
catalog actually yields models with both values;
`context/fragments_test.go` covers the pointer semantics (nil -> bundled asset,
explicit empty -> disabled) and the asset shape.

Verification: `go build ./...`; `go vet ./context ./appserver` clean (the only
vet noise is the pre-existing `model/responses_agent.go` lock copy warning);
`gofmt -l` clean; `go test ./model ./context ./app ./parity ./appserver -count=1`
green, plus `CODEX_GO_MODELS_JSON=<rust models.json> go test ./model -run
BundledCatalog` green.

Residual (recorded): the catalog's `model_messages.guardian_v2`
(`classifier_instructions`, `review_threshold_basis_points`, `transcript`, ...)
is still unparsed and unconsumed in Go, and `persistent_instructions` is only
consulted by the persistent-mode fragment (Rust also mirrors it into the
persistent-mode world-state snapshot, which Go rebuilds per turn).

### Parity gate: vendored persistent-mode prompt

- `parity/rust_persistent_mode_prompt_test.go` (new):
  `TestRustPersistentModePromptMatchesGo` compares the vendored
  `context/templates/persistent_mode.md` with Rust's
  `core/assets/persistent_mode.md` (read via `git show` so a Windows autocrlf
  checkout cannot mask drift; the Go copy is CRLF-normalized). Re-adding
  `TestRustPersistentModePromptMatchesGo` to the `features`-style static layer
  keeps the vendored prompt honest if upstream edits it.

## Non-TUI core: managed residency on the model catalog (request + identity)

Rust's `read_default_client_residency_requirement` /
`set_default_client_residency_requirement` install the managed
`enforce_residency` requirement process-wide (app-server config manager,
initialize processor, `codex exec`, TUI startup), and
`model-provider::enforce_managed_residency` then adds the internal residency
header (`x-openai-internal-codex-residency`) to the API provider - which is used
by ordinary requests, the **models catalog request** (`models_endpoint.rs:95`)
and the **catalog identity digest** (`models_identity.rs:19`).

Go applied residency only to the responses runner (`agent.Residency`). The
catalog fetch carried no residency header, and the cache identity ignored the
requirement, so a residency change did not invalidate `models_cache.json` and a
managed catalog request looked unmanaged.

- `model/provider_info.go`: `APIProvider.ApplyManagedResidency(residency)`
  mirrors `enforce_managed_residency` (trimmed; empty is a no-op; otherwise it
  sets `ResidencyHeaderName`).
- `model/provider.go`: `ConfiguredProvider` carries the resolved requirement
  (`SetManagedResidency`), `APIProvider()` applies it, `ModelsManager` passes it
  to the identity, and `CreateRuntimeProviderWithResidency` constructs a
  provider with the requirement already resolved.
- `model/models_identity.go`: `ModelsCatalogIdentity` takes the residency and
  applies it to the routed provider before digesting, so the header participates
  in the identity exactly like Rust.
- `appserver/runtime_router.go`: the account-scoped catalog builder is now a
  named `buildAccountScopedModelsManager(codexHome, read, requirements)`;
  it resolves the requirement from the config service and enforces it on both
  the request and the identity. `appserver/agent_runtime.go` passes the
  per-turn requirement to the provider it builds (new
  `managedResidencyFromRequirements`).
- `exec/exec.go`: `agentForRun` and the image-generation provider resolve the
  requirement from the loaded config, pass it to the provider, and set
  `agent.Residency` - mirroring Rust's exec startup install.

Tests: `model/models_identity_test.go` adds the residency-sensitive identity
vector; `model/provider_residency_test.go` asserts the header is absent without
a requirement and reaches the catalog request (and `APIProvider().Headers`) with
one, including trimming; `appserver/runtime_router_account_models_test.go`
drives `buildAccountScopedModelsManager` against a stub catalog endpoint with
and without `ConfigRequirements.EnforceResidency`;
`exec/residency_test.go` covers `agentForRun` residency plus the requirement
lookup.

Verification: `go build ./...`; `gofmt -l` clean; `go vet` clean apart from the
pre-existing `model/responses_agent.go` and `exec/agent_controller.go` copylocks
warnings; `go test ./model ./exec ./app ./parity ./doctor ./appserver ./tool
./turn -count=1` green.

Residual: the remaining `CreateRuntimeProviderForID` call sites in `exec`
(web-search provider construction), `doctor`, and the app-server image/realtime/
web-search runtimes do not resolve a requirement of their own; they cover
provider metadata or already pass through the per-turn requirement.

## Non-TUI core: provider capabilities RPC, Bedrock web search, parallel tool calls

Three related catalog-vs-provider divergences found while diffing Go's
`ModelInfo` against Rust's (`protocol/src/openai_models.rs` has no
`supports_parallel_tool_calls` field, and Rust's
`modelProvider/capabilities/read` never consults the catalog):

- **`modelProvider/capabilities/read`** (Rust
  `config_processor.rs::model_provider_capabilities_read`): Rust loads the
  latest config, builds the configured provider and returns
  `provider.capabilities()`. Go derived the three flags from the *default
  catalog model* (`input_modalities`, `supports_parallel_tool_calls`,
  `supports_search_tool`), so a sparse/custom catalog reported wrong
  capabilities. `appserver/runtime_router.go` now resolves the provider from the
  config and reports its capabilities, keeping the catalog-derived
  `ModelService.ProviderCapabilities` only as a fallback when no config service
  is attached.
- **Bedrock web search** (`AmazonBedrockModelProvider::capabilities`):
  `web_search` is true on the Mantle endpoint (`amazon-bedrock`) and false on the
  Runtime endpoint (`amazon-bedrock-runtime`). Go hardcoded false for both;
  `AmazonBedrockProvider.Capabilities` now keys it on
  `AmazonBedrockProviderName`.
- **`parallel_tool_calls` request flag**: Rust's `build_prompt` sets
  `parallel_tool_calls: true` unconditionally and the request applies only
  `&& !use_responses_lite`; the catalog flag Go consulted
  (`ModelInfo.SupportsParallelToolCalls`, absent from Rust's struct) is ignored
  upstream. `appserver/turn_runtime.go` and `exec/exec.go` now gate on
  responses-lite alone.

Tests: `appserver/runtime_router_test.go` adds the three Rust
`model_provider_capabilities_read.rs` vectors (default OpenAI, Bedrock Mantle,
Bedrock Runtime) against a real config service;
`model/bedrock_capabilities_test.go` pins both Bedrock endpoints;
`model/provider_test.go`'s existing Bedrock capability assertion is updated to
the Rust-faithful Mantle value.

Verification: `go build ./...`; `gofmt -l` clean; `go test ./model ./appserver
./exec ./tool ./app ./parity ./doctor -count=1` green.

Residual: `ModelInfo.SupportsParallelToolCalls` remains parsed for the
catalog-derived fallback in `ModelService.ProviderCapabilities`; Rust has no such
field, so the fallback stays a Go-only convenience for routers without config.

## Non-TUI core: reject transport-mismatched `mcp_servers` tables

Rust converts every `mcp_servers.<name>` table through
`TryFrom<RawMcpServerConfig> for McpServerConfig` (`config/src/mcp_types.rs`),
which rejects fields that do not belong to the table's transport - `url` on a
stdio server, `bearer_token`/`args`/`env`/`env_vars`/`cwd` on an HTTP server,
`oauth`/`auth`/`http_headers`/`http_headers_helper` on stdio, an empty
`http_headers_helper`, a helper with a non-local `environment_id`, or a table
with neither `command` nor `url` ("invalid transport"). Go previously chose a
transport by URL presence and silently ignored the conflicting fields, so a
mistyped `[mcp_servers.x]` block loaded with the wrong transport.

- `config/mcp_server_transport.go` (new): `validateMCPServerTransportFields`
  ports Rust's `throw_if_set` matrix with the exact messages, treating a
  present-but-null key as unset (serde `Option<T>`) and any other present value
  (including an empty string/map) as set. Errors are prefixed with
  `mcp_servers.<name>:` for the offending server.
- `config/config.go`: the check runs in the always-on section of
  `LoadEffectiveWithOptions` (next to the `mcp_oauth_credentials_store` and
  memories-version checks), so `codex exec`, the app server and the TUI all fail
  a bad server table at load time like Rust.

Tests: `config/mcp_server_transport_test.go` covers the full rule matrix
(valid stdio/HTTP tables, explicit null as unset, empty map as set, each
mismatched field, empty helper, remote-environment helper, missing transport)
and the load boundary (`LoadEffectiveWithOptions` rejects a `command` + `url`
table and accepts a valid two-server config).

Verification: `go build ./...`; `gofmt -l` clean; `go test ./config ./app
./exec ./doctor ./parity ./model ./tool ./turn ./session ./appserver -count=1`
green.

Residual: the same rules are not applied to plugin-contributed or thread-config
`mcp_servers` maps that bypass the config loader (`mcp.ServerConfigFromValues`
stays lenient), and the ema-specific `oauth.authorization_server_issuer` rule
keeps its existing separate validator.

### Same item, nested shapes: `tools.<tool>` and `env_vars`

Rust's nested MCP config types also reject bad shapes at deserialization:
`McpServerToolConfig` denies unknown fields (`approval_mode`,
`output_token_limit`), and `McpServerEnvVar::Config` denies unknown fields and
validates `source` (`local`/`remote`). Go ignored all three.

- `config/mcp_server_transport.go`: `validateMCPServerNestedFields` (run
  alongside the transport check in the always-on load section) rejects an
  unknown per-tool field (`unknown configuration field
  \`mcp_servers.<srv>.tools.<tool>.<key>\``), an unknown `env_vars` entry field,
  and an unsupported `env_vars` source (Rust's
  "unsupported env_vars source `X`; expected `local` or `remote`").
- `config/mcp_server_transport_test.go` extends the matrix with valid nested
  tools/env_vars, an unknown tool field, an unknown env-vars field, and a bad
  source.

Verification: `go test ./config ./app ./exec ./doctor ./parity ./tool ./turn
./session ./model -count=1` green; `go test ./appserver -count=1` green on
re-run (the first run hit the pre-existing load-sensitive notification-timeout
flakes - `TestRuntimeRouterTurnStartUsesThreadFeatureOverridesForRequestUserInputToolDescriptionLikeRust`
this time - which passes in isolation and with `-count=3`).

## Non-TUI core: per-tool parallel-safety flags (`supports_parallel_tool_calls`)

Rust gates each tool call on `ToolHandler::supports_parallel_tool_calls`, whose
trait default is **false** (`tools/src/tool_executor.rs`); only exec_command,
write_stdin, test_sync, view_image, tool_search and the three MCP-resource
handlers override it with true, and
`ToolRouter::tool_supports_parallel` treats an unknown tool as false. Diffing
Go's `Spec.Parallel` values against that default found six mismatches:

- `turn/dynamic_tool_runtime.go`: dynamic (host-provided) tools were parallel;
  Rust's `DynamicToolHandler` has no override -> false.
- `turn/skills_tools.go`: the skills extension tools were parallel; Rust's
  `ext/skills` handlers have no override -> false.
- `tool/plugin_install.go`: `list_available_plugins_to_install` was parallel;
  Rust's handler returns false -> false.
- `agent/tools.go` / `agent/tools_v2.go`: `spawn_agent` (v1 and v2) was
  parallel; Rust's multi-agent handlers have no override -> false.
- `turn/mcp_resource_handlers.go`: `list_mcp_resources`,
  `list_mcp_resource_templates` and `read_mcp_resource` were serial; Rust
  overrides them with true -> parallel.

`turn/tools_parallel_test.go` gains
`TestBuiltinToolParallelFlagsLikeRust`, which builds the default registry (plus
an MCP server so the resource and skills tools register) and pins each tool's
`Router.SupportsParallel` value to Rust's.

Verification: `go build ./...`; `gofmt -l` clean; `go test ./agent ./turn
./tool ./app ./parity ./appserver ./exec -count=1` green.

Residual: `web_search` (Go true) and `image_generation` (Go false) are
Go-registered specs for tools Rust declares as hosted Responses tools without a
registry handler, so there is no Rust `supports_parallel_tool_calls` value to
match; both are left unchanged and noted here.

## Non-TUI core: stale vendored review rubric (Rust #34637)

A content diff of Go's vendored prompt assets against their Rust sources
(memories templates, packaged defaults, review rubric) found one drift: Go's
`review/rubric.md` was missing Rust's
`## Repository Rule Attribution` section that `81de4f251c` (#34637 "Attribute
review findings to repository rules") added to
`prompts/templates/review/rubric.md`. Both the TUI review flow and
`codex exec review` therefore asked the reviewer to report findings without
Rust's rules for attributing them to project instruction files, verifying the
supporting line range, and emitting a compact reference - and without the
"do not omit ordinary findings or invent findings" guard.

- `review/rubric.md` is now byte-identical (LF) to Rust's file (+8 lines).
- `parity/rust_review_rubric_test.go` (new): `TestRustReviewRubricMatchesGo`
  compares the vendored rubric with `prompts/templates/review/rubric.md` read
  through `git show` (CRLF-normalized) and asserts the rule-attribution heading
  is present, so the asset cannot silently drift again.

Verified clean in the same sweep: `config/defaults.toml` (byte-identical) and
all seven vendored memories templates (`ad_hoc_instructions`, `stage_one_system`
and `_v2`, `stage_one_input` and `_v2`, `consolidation` and `_v2`).

Verification: `go build ./...`; `gofmt -l` clean; `go test ./review ./exec
./app ./parity ./tool ./appserver -count=1` green.

## Decision required: the fallback base instructions (`models-manager/prompt.md`)

Sweeping Go's vendored prompt assets against Rust found one **unresolved
product-identity difference** that should be decided rather than silently
changed:

- Rust's fallback base instructions are `models-manager/prompt.md` (21,178
  bytes; "You are a coding agent running in the Codex CLI, ..."), embedded as
  `BASE_INSTRUCTIONS` and used by `models_manager::model_info_from_slug` and the
  bundled catalog's `instructions_template`.
- Go's equivalent is the hand-written `model.BaseInstructions` in
  `model/catalog.go` (a much shorter document whose first line is "You are
  gcode, a coding agent based on GPT-5."), used by `localModelMessages()`,
  `fallbackBundledModelsResponse()` and `ModelInstructions` when a model has no
  catalog `model_messages`.
- The Go text is stylistically an upstream catalog `instructions_template`
  (older `base_instructions`) with the product name rewritten to "gcode"; the
  port deliberately rebrands (module `codex_go`, binary `codex.exe`, home
  `.gcode`), while the README states the goal is to align with the Rust CLI.
- Impact: with the Rust catalog present (development checkouts) the catalog's
  `instructions_template` wins and the difference is invisible; a release build
  that falls back to `fallbackBundledModelsResponse()` sends the Go text
  instead of upstream's 21 KB prompt, so the reviewer/agent guidance (including
  the upstream `# AGENTS.md spec` and `# Tool Guidelines` sections) differs.

No test pins the Go text, so adopting upstream's prompt is a mechanical change
(embed `prompt.md`), but it replaces the port's own agent identity text. This
note records the evidence and leaves the choice to the operator instead of
making a unilateral product change.

Verified clean in the same sweep: the seven vendored memories templates, the
packaged `config/defaults.toml`, and (after this pass's fix) `review/rubric.md`.

### Parity gate: vendored permission-prompt templates

- `sandbox/permission_prompt_templates_parity_test.go` (new):
  `TestPermissionPromptTemplatesMatchRust` compares all seven Go constants in
  `sandbox/permission_prompt_templates.go` with
  `codex-rs/prompts/templates/permissions/{approval_policy,sandbox_mode}/*.md`
  (CRLF-normalized; sandbox-mode templates `TrimRight`-ed to mirror Rust's
  `Template::parse(<template>.trim_end())`, approval templates compared raw).
  All seven currently match byte for byte, so the permission/approval prompt
  text is now pinned as well as the review rubric and persistent-mode prompt.

Verification: `gofmt -l` clean; `go vet ./sandbox` clean; `go test ./sandbox/...
-count=1` green.

## Non-TUI core: adopt Rust's base instructions (`models-manager/prompt.md`)

Closes the decision item recorded in the previous pass. Go's fallback base
instructions were a shorter, rebranded text ("You are gcode, a coding agent based
on GPT-5.") derived from a retired catalog `base_instructions`; upstream's
fallback is `models-manager/prompt.md` (21 KB, `BASE_INSTRUCTIONS`), which
`model_info_from_slug` and the bundled catalog's `instructions_template` use.
Since the repository's stated goal is alignment with the Rust CLI, Go now ships
the upstream document.

- `model/prompt.md` (new, vendored verbatim from Rust, LF-normalized) plus
  `model/catalog.go`: `BaseInstructions` is now an embedded `//go:embed
  prompt.md` variable instead of a hand-written const, so every fallback path
  (`localModelMessages`, `fallbackBundledModelsResponse`, `OSSModelCatalog`,
  `ModelInstructions` when a model has no `model_messages`) uses upstream's
  prompt, including its `# AGENTS.md spec` and `# Tool Guidelines` sections.
- `model/catalog_test.go`: `TestDefaultBaseInstructionsRequirePreambleBeforeTools`
  now asserts the upstream guidance ("Before making tool calls, send a brief
  preamble", "### Preamble messages", "immediately about to be done next",
  "# Tool Guidelines", "# AGENTS.md spec").
- `parity/rust_base_instructions_test.go` (new):
  `TestRustBaseInstructionsMatchGo` compares the vendored prompt with
  `models-manager/prompt.md` read through `git show` (CRLF-normalized) so the
  asset cannot drift.

Note: this replaces the port's own agent identity line in the fallback prompt
with upstream's wording; the catalog-provided `instructions_template` (the
common case) was already upstream text and is unchanged.

Verification: `go build ./...`; `gofmt -l` clean; `go test ./model ./app ./exec
./parity ./appserver -count=1` green.

### Parity gate: compact and review-exit prompt templates

- `parity/rust_prompt_templates_test.go` (new):
  `TestRustCompactAndReviewTemplatesMatchGo` compares
  `compact.SummarizationPrompt` / `compact.SummaryPrefix` and
  `review.RenderReviewExitInterrupted()` / `RenderReviewExitSuccess("{{results}}")`
  with Rust's `prompts/templates/{compact/prompt.md,compact/summary_prefix.md,
  review/exit_interrupted.xml,review/exit_success.xml}` (git show, CRLF
  normalized, newline-trimmed to match Rust's template parse). All four match,
  so every vendored prompt document in `prompts/templates` is now pinned except
  the voice-owned `realtime/*` templates.

Verification: `gofmt -l` clean; `go test ./compact ./review ./parity -count=1`
green.

### mcp_servers validation on the request-override boundary

`config.ValidateMCPServerValues` is now exported and called where the app server
applies `thread/start` / `turn/start` `config` overrides
(`effectiveConfigForThreadStart`, `effectiveConfigForTurn`), so a
transport-mismatched `mcp_servers` entry in a request override fails the request
like Rust's typed config conversion instead of loading with the wrong transport.

Tests: `appserver/runtime_router_test.go`
`TestRuntimeRouterRejectsTransportMismatchedMCPOverridesLikeRust` (bad and valid
`mcp_servers` overrides for both request builders) and
`config/mcp_server_transport_test.go` `TestValidateMCPServerValuesLikeRust`.

Verification: `go build ./...`; `go test ./config ./app ./exec ./appserver
-count=1` green.

Residual (recorded): the thread-lifecycle and skills override paths
(`applyThreadLifecycleMCPConfig`, `skillMetadataForMCPRequirements`) and
plugin-contributed server configs (`mcp.ServerConfigFromValues`) still bypass the
check, because changing those signatures ripples through non-error callers.

## Non-TUI core: Guardian v2 `max_tool_call_lag` resolution

Auditing the `guardian_v2` catalog messages (previously recorded as N/A) found a
real, reachable gap: the stale-score bound never reached the reviewer.

- Rust's `DEFAULT_MAX_TOOL_CALL_LAG` is **2**
  (`ext/guardian-v2/src/async_scorer/config.rs`, #39001), resolved as
  `configured.max_tool_call_lag.or(model_defaults.max_tool_call_lag)` then
  `unwrap_or(DEFAULT)`. Go hard-coded 3 in both
  `config.GuardianV2MaxToolCallLag` and `defaultGuardianMaxToolCallLag`.
- `config.GuardianV2MaxToolCallLag()` had **no production caller**, so
  `[features.guardianv2].max_tool_call_lag` was parsed but ignored, and
  `modelGuardianReviewer.SetMaxToolCallLag` was dead code - the reviewer always
  used its hard-coded default.

Changes:

- `model/catalog.go`: `ModelMessages` gains `GuardianV2 *GuardianV2ModelConfig`
  (parsed in both the struct tag and the custom `UnmarshalJSON`), with Rust's
  scalar fields (`classifier_instructions`, `review_threshold_basis_points`,
  `max_tool_call_lag`, `reasoning_effort`, `max_action_tokens`,
  `max_classifier_instruction_tokens`) and the `transcript` document retained
  raw. Only `max_tool_call_lag` has a Go consumer; the classifier-only fields
  stay N/A with Go's missing async scorer.
- `config/config.go`: `GuardianV2MaxToolCallLag()` now reports whether the
  config set the value (`(int, bool)`, matching Rust's `Option<usize>`), so the
  model default can fill in between it and the final default.
- `appserver/guardian_reviewer.go`: the default is 2, the reviewer resolves the
  bound per review through a new `maxToolCallLagFor` callback with
  `maxToolCallLagValue` falling back to `SetMaxToolCallLag` then the default.
- `appserver/agent_runtime.go`: `guardianMaxToolCallLagForTurn` resolves the
  turn's config and model info, and `guardianMaxToolCallLag` ports Rust's
  precedence (configured -> catalog -> 2).

Tests: `config/config_test.go` (presence semantics + default unset),
`model/catalog_messages_test.go`
`TestModelMessagesParsesGuardianV2LikeRust`, and
`appserver/guardian_reviewer_test.go` `TestGuardianMaxToolCallLagLikeRust`
(precedence and the reviewer's resolution).

Verification: `go build ./...`; `gofmt -l` clean; `go test ./model ./config
./appserver ./app ./exec ./parity ./doctor -count=1` green.

## TUI pass: task-tools namespace on the prompt-edit branch

The backtrack prompt-edit path built its own app-server client, so a branched
thread never carried the session's task-tools namespace (the fresh thread fell
back to the callback transport, whose registration dies with the edit, and the
fork got no `mcp_servers.codex_tui` override).

- `app/prompt_edit.go`: `interactiveRemotePromptEditHandler` takes the session's
  `taskToolsMCPHost` (variadic, so existing call sites keep compiling); the edit
  client attaches to the host for the duration of the edit (template from
  `remoteThreadStartParams`, suspended when done), `StartFreshThread` applies the
  transport (`applyTaskToolTransport`, so the MCP transport drops
  `dynamicTools`) and persists the capability marker - only for the hosted MCP
  transport, because the callback transport registers the namespace on a
  connection that closes with the edit - and `ForkThread` merges the
  `mcp_servers.codex_tui` override into `ThreadForkParams.config` (Rust
  `ThreadToolTransport::configure_mcp`).
- `app/remote_tui.go`: the `OnPromptEdit` wiring passes the session host.

Test: `app/prompt_edit_test.go`
`TestRemotePromptEditCarriesTaskToolsNamespaceLikeRust` drives start and fork
against a scripted app server and asserts the fresh thread's
`mcp_servers.codex_tui` override (and no `dynamicTools`), the persisted
capability marker, and the fork's config override.

Verification: `go build ./...`; `go vet ./app` clean; `go test ./app ./parity
-count=1` green.

## TUI disposition (evidence): background-thread server requests

Rust buffers a server request (approval, user input, elicitation) that arrives for
a thread the TUI is not viewing in
`agents_overview.dispatched_requests[thread_id]`, removes it on
`ServerRequestResolved`, replays it when the user navigates to that thread
(`replay_agents_overview_requests`), and surfaces the pending count in the
dashboard. Go answers every server request inline on the connection that
received it (`remoteAppServerTUIClient.respondServerRequest`).

This is **architecture-different, not a bounded port**: Go's remote TUI creates
one app-server client per turn and closes it when the turn ends
(`runInteractiveRemoteTurn`), while Rust's `AppServerSession` keeps a single
connection for the whole session. A request buffered for a background thread
could therefore never be answered later in Go - the connection that owns the
thread is gone - so deferring would hang the background turn instead of
surfacing it. Because Go starts background task threads through the current
turn's client (the dynamic tools call `thread/start` on it), their requests
arrive while that connection is still open and are surfaced immediately, which
is safe if more intrusive than Rust's dashboard badge. Porting Rust's behavior
requires the session-scoped-client refactor (one long-lived TUI app-server
connection) and is recorded here rather than half-implemented.

## Non-TUI core: fallback model metadata (`model_info_from_slug`)

Comparing Go's `ModelInfoFromSlug` with Rust's
`models_manager::model_info_from_slug` found three field drifts in the fallback
descriptor used for unknown/missing slugs:

- `ShellType`: Rust `UnifiedExec`, Go unset -> now `"unified_exec"`.
- `IncludeSkillsUsageInstructions`: Rust false, Go true -> now false (the turn
  runtime reads this per turn, so unknown models wrongly received the skills
  usage instructions).
- `SupportsReasoningSummaries`: Rust true (serde default), Go false -> now true
  (`responses_agent` omits the reasoning summary parameter when false).

Test: `model/catalog_test.go` `TestModelInfoFromSlugMatchesRustFallback` pins the
whole fallback descriptor (shell type, visibility/api/priority, the three usage
instruction flags, reasoning-summary support, the capability flags, text web
search, the 10k-byte truncation policy, the 272k context window and the fallback
flag).

Verification: `go build ./...`; `go test ./model ./app ./exec ./parity
-count=1` green (the appserver suite hit the documented load-sensitive
`TestRuntimeRouterMCPEventStreamStartStopForwardsAndScopesLikeRust` flake, which
passes in isolation).

## Non-TUI core: account-bound usage metadata (`account/rateLimits/read`)

Rust's `GetAccountRateLimitsResponse`
(`app-server-protocol/src/protocol/v2/account.rs`) carries three fields Go
omitted entirely, so the app server dropped the backend's usage banner and the
ordinary-usage decision:

- `ordinaryUsageAllowed` (the backend's ordinary included-usage decision),
- `accountId` (the account the usage read belongs to),
- `rateLimitUpsell` (the backend-owned banner payload, nested snake_case kept
  verbatim; the source of the TUI's inline banner).

Rust fills them from the usage read
(`backend-client/src/client/rate_limit_resets.rs`): `ordinary_usage_allowed` is
the primary rate limit's `allowed`, `account_id`/`user_id`/`rate_limit_upsell`
come from the payload, and `account_processor.rs` only exposes the ordinary
decision and the banner when the read belongs to the active non-FedRAMP account
(`!is_fedramp_account() && account_id == auth.account_id() && user_id ==
auth.chatgpt_user_id()`), reporting `account_id` regardless.

- `chatgptapi`: `RateLimitStatusPayload` gains `account_id`/`user_id`/
  `rate_limit_upsell`; `RateLimitsWithResetCredits` gains
  `OrdinaryUsageAllowed`/`AccountID`/`UserID`/`RateLimitUpsell`, filled by
  `ordinaryUsageAllowedFromPayload` (the payload's `rate_limit.allowed`).
- `auth`: `GetAccountRateLimitsResponse` gains the three wire fields (explicit
  nulls, matching Rust) and `AccountManager` caches them via the new
  `SetRateLimitExtras`; `IsFedrampAccount` mirrors Rust's
  `CodexAuth::is_fedramp_account` for token/claim-backed auth.
- `appserver/runtime_router.go`: `accountBoundRateLimitExtras` ports the
  active-account readiness filter and feeds the account manager.

Tests: `appserver/runtime_router_test.go`
`TestAccountBoundRateLimitExtrasLikeRust` (matching account, mismatched account
id, FedRAMP, missing user id, and the always-reported account id).

Verification: `go build ./...`; `gofmt -l` clean; `go test ./auth ./chatgptapi
./app ./parity ./appserver -count=1` green.

Residual: the TUI still does not render the banner or consume
`ordinaryUsageAllowed` (the inline `ActionableBanner` plus the
`backend_banner_fallback` flow remain unported); this pass lands the wire
contract and the app-server filtering they need.

## Non-TUI core: two v2 schema fields Go omitted

A mechanical diff of Rust's generated v2 JSON schemas
(`app-server-protocol/schema/json/v2/*.json`, 271 types) against Go's struct
json tags found two types where Go was missing a Rust field (the rest of the 51
reported differences are Go-side extras/internal fields or same-name structs in
other packages):

- `AccountLoginCompletedNotification.onboardingEntrypoint` (Rust
  `account.rs`, `DesktopOnboardingEntrypoint` = `"life_sciences"`): Go's
  notification lacked the field, so the v2 payload did not match the schema.
  `auth` now declares it (always serialized, null like Rust when absent) with
  the `DesktopOnboardingEntrypointLifeSciences` constant.
- `ExternalAgentConfigImportHistoriesReadResponse.connectors` (required in
  Rust): Go's response only had `data`. `config` now declares
  `Connectors []ExternalAgentImportedConnectorCandidate` (source
  `remoteMcpServersConfig`), always serialized as an array, and
  `ImportedConnectorCandidates` ports Rust `read_imported_connector_candidates`
  - group the ledger's connector names by source path, keep one spelling per
  source, count the sessions referencing each connector, sort by name - wired
  into `ConfigService.ImportHistories`.

Tests: `config/external_session_ledger_checkpoint_test.go`
`TestImportedConnectorCandidatesLikeRust` (grouping/dedup/counts/order) and
`TestImportHistoriesResponseIncludesConnectorsLikeRust` (the required array is
present, never null); `auth/account_test.go`
`TestAccountLoginCompletedNotificationIncludesOnboardingEntrypointLikeRust`
(null default and the life-sciences value).

Verification: `go build ./...`; `gofmt -l` clean; `go test ./auth ./config
./appserver ./app ./parity ./doctor -count=1` green.

Residuals: Go does not source the onboarding entrypoint (its login flow has no
desktop login-callback entrypoint state, so the field stays null, matching
Rust's CLI flows), and Rust's ledger also counts *detected* (unimported)
connector records (`detected_connector_records`) which Go does not persist yet.

### `turn/start` per-turn service tier (`serviceTierForTurn`)

The schema diff (`TurnStartParams`) surfaced one more real wire gap: Rust's
`service_tier_for_turn` ("Override the service tier only when this request
starts a new turn. Use \"default\" for standard speed. Omitted or null inherits
the thread's tier. Does not change the thread's tier or a turn being steered.")
had no Go equivalent, so a client setting it was silently ignored.

- `turn/api.go`: `TurnStartParams.ServiceTierForTurn` (`serviceTierForTurn`).
- `appserver/turn_runtime.go`: `appServiceTierForTurn` prefers the per-turn
  override, resolving it through `model.ServiceTierForRequest` (so "fast"
  normalizes to `priority` and "default" clears the tier) and leaving the
  thread/config tier untouched. Go's steering path (`turn/steer` via the steer
  mailbox) never goes through this builder, matching Rust's "does not change a
  turn being steered".

Test: `appserver/guardian_reviewer_test.go`
`TestAppServiceTierForTurnHonorsPerTurnOverrideLikeRust` (override wins over the
config tier, "fast" normalizes, "default" clears).

Verification: `go build ./...`; `go test ./turn ./app ./parity -count=1` green;
`go test ./appserver -count=1` green apart from the documented load-sensitive
`TestRuntimeRouterMCPEventStreamStartStopForwardsAndScopesLikeRust` flake
(passes in isolation).

### `thread/approveGuardianDeniedAction` param shape (Go-only `actionId` removed)

The schema diff also flagged Go's approve-denied-action params: Rust's
`ThreadApproveGuardianDeniedActionParams` is `{ thread_id, event }` with the
event *required* (`JsonValue`, deserialized into a typed
`GuardianAssessmentEvent`), while Go declared `event` optional and accepted a
Go-only `actionId` fallback that the handler never read (the remote TUI sent
both).

- `appserver/protocol.go`: `ActionID` is gone, `Event` is the required `event`
  field, and `Validate` requires a non-null event.
- `appserver/guardian_reviewer.go`: a non-object event is rejected with
  `invalid Guardian denial event: expected an object`, mirroring Rust's typed
  deserialization failure instead of silently no-opping.
- `app/remote_tui.go`: the remote TUI sends only the serialized event.

Tests: `appserver/guardian_reviewer_test.go`
`TestApproveGuardianDeniedActionRejectsNonObjectEventLikeRust` (missing, null,
scalar, array) plus the existing missing-event router test.

Verification: `go build ./...`; `go test ./app ./parity -count=1` green;
`go test ./appserver -count=1` green.

`a592c38c16` (10 commits). The local Rust checkout (`D:\qax\reagent\dev\git\codex`)
was fast-forwarded to the new HEAD so the parity gates and the L0 freeze pin the
current target.

### Non-TUI core: `send_message_to_user_async` feature gate (#45124)

Rust adds the disabled-by-default `send_message_to_user_async` feature
(`Stage::UnderDevelopment`) so a root agent can use the free-form async message
tool without model-catalog support, and changes `add_core_utility_tools` to

    !is_non_root_agent && (features.enabled(SendMessageToUserAsync) || catalog has "send_message_to_user_async")

- `features/features.go`: the new `send_message_to_user_async` registry entry,
  placed between `send_async_message` (removed) and `guardian_approval` to match
  Rust's `FEATURES` order (the `parity` L0 registry check now passes against the
  new Rust HEAD).
- `appserver/runtime_router.go`: new `freeformAsyncToolAvailable` helper
  (root agent AND (catalog opt-in OR feature)) plus `turnThreadIsSubagent`, which
  ports `SessionSource::is_non_root_agent()` using `runtimeRecordIsSubagent` and
  fails open when the store/record is unavailable. The catalog path now also
  honors the root-agent gate (previously Go offered the tool to catalog
  subagents, which Rust already forbade).
- `appserver/async_message_test.go`: `TestFreeformAsyncToolAvailableLikeRust`
  covers root+catalog, root+feature, root+both, root+neither, the legacy
  `send_user_message_async`/`request_user_input_async` names, and the three
  subagent cases from Rust's `freeform_async_message_requires_root_and_catalog_or_feature_opt_in`.
- Committed as `1d6c13e`.

### Non-TUI core: content-based agent-message token estimate (#45094)

Rust charges each response item from its model-visible content. Go's live
compaction estimator (`compact.EstimateItemTokens`) already counts text content
for most items, but its structured `agent_message` branch charged
`len(item.Raw)` - the serialized envelope with keys, an id, quoting, and
escaping.

- `compact/compact.go`: the agent-message branch now sums author + recipient +
  each `input_text` block + the encrypted-content plaintext approximation
  (`encoded*9` div-ceil 16), matching Rust's `ResponseItem::AgentMessage` arm;
  the now-dead `agentMessageEncryptedContents` helper was removed.
- `compact/compact_test.go`: the encrypted-agent-message expectation is pinned
  to the content sum, and a new regression test proves extra envelope metadata
  (an id and a metadata block with escaped text) does not move the estimate.
- Committed as `02428a0`.
- Residual (recorded): Go's `compact.Item` does not carry `name`, `namespace`,
  or `call_id`, so Rust's `FunctionCall`/`FunctionCallOutput`/`CustomToolCallOutput`
  content arms (which add those metadata strings to the output body) cannot be
  reproduced without extending the compact item shape; the non-agent-message
  items still route through the Unicode-aware `EstimateTextTokens(ItemText)`
  heuristic (a deliberate Go design, plan_20260722) rather than Rust's bytes/4.
  The dead `context.EstimateItemTokens`/`EstimateTokenCount` (JSON-serialized,
  unused) was left as-is; Rust's plaintext-reasoning exclusion and image/audio
  modality estimates remain unmodeled in the compact path.

Note: the Astra sparkle composer item recorded earlier as the "largest remaining

### TUI pass: multiline feedback notes stop submitting early (#45116)

- `tui/tea/feedback.go`: the live `/feedback` note modal now carries a
  `bottompane.PasteBurst`. A rapid unbracketed paste keeps its newlines (and a
  tab) in the note, Enter inserts a newline while the burst window is open and
  submits only once it lapses, a bracketed (`message.Paste`) or modified-Enter
  input clears the burst so a following Enter submits immediately, and other
  keys reset the burst. `Options.DisablePasteBurst` still short-circuits the
  burst so existing flow tests stay deterministic.
- `tui/bottom_pane/feedback_view.go`: the parity `FeedbackNoteView` gains
  `HandleKeyAt(key, now)` (mirroring Rust's `handle_key_event_at`) with the same
  burst rules; `Paste` clears the burst. Named keys (backspace, arrows) do not
  track the burst.
- Tests: `tui/tea/model_test.go` adds the unbracketed-burst and bracketed-paste
  submissions (`TestModelFeedbackNoteMultilinePasteBurstDoesNotSubmitEarly`,
  `TestModelFeedbackNoteBracketedPasteSubmitsImmediately`); the parity view adds
  `TestFeedbackNoteViewMultilinePasteBurstMatchesRust` and
  `TestFeedbackNoteViewExplicitPasteClearsBurstMatchesRust`.
- Verification: `go build ./...`; `gofmt -l` clean; `go test ./tui/... -count=1`
  green. Committed as `b2df43d`.

### TUI pass: live prose preview before a newline (#45135)

- `tui/streaming/controller.go`: the stream core now carries a disposable
  `prosePreview` mirroring Rust `streaming/prose_preview.rs`. `pushDelta`
  refreshes it after every delta (resetting on any newline), `currentTailLines`
  appends its lines after the committed-source tail, `setWidth` re-renders it,
  and `reset` clears it. It is bounded to the most recent 8 KiB cut at a rune
  boundary with a leading ellipsis, keeps the last safe text once a pipe reveals
  table structure, and defers to the existing indented/quoted/fence holdback.
- `tui/streaming/table_holdback.go`: new `AllowsProsePreview()` mirrors Rust's
  `!in_table && !previous_line.is_header && fence == Outside` through the shared
  scanner state.
- Tests: `TestStreamControllerPreviewsUnterminatedProse` (visible before the
  newline, out of the stable queue, rendered once on commit),
  `TestStreamControllerProsePreviewBoundedAtRuneBoundary` (8 KiB ellipsis, valid
  UTF-8), `TestStreamControllerProsePreviewRetainsSafeTextOnPipe`, and
  `TestStreamControllerProsePreviewReflowsOnResize`.
- Verification: `go build ./...`; `gofmt -l` clean; `go test ./tui/... -count=1`
  green. Committed as `0e9bbb5`.
  - Residual: the Rust change also updates TUI snapshots
    (`unterminated_agent_prose_preview`, `unterminated_plan_prose_preview`,
    status/layout replay) because the chatwidget's active-tail rendering
    changed. Go renders the same tail through `ActiveTail`; no Go snapshot is
    pinned to the old hidden behavior, so no Go snapshot needed updating.

### Triage: thread-title cancellation (#45108) - N/A for Go's architecture

Rust cancels pending title-generation requests after a manual rename, interrupts
their temporary turns, unsubscribes the temporary threads, ignores late events,
and shows a spinner-only "generating" state. Go has no hidden title-generation
thread or pending-request state at all: the local interactive TUI derives a
provisional title synchronously from the first prompt
(`app.interactiveAutoThreadTitle`) and the remote TUI never requests a generated
title. There is therefore nothing to cancel, interrupt, or unsubscribe, and no
`renaming...` placeholder to remove, so the commit is N/A for the Go port.

### TUI pass: structured recaps and the shared prompt (#45090/#45089)

- `tui/app/recap.go`: replaced the 900-byte, single-string recap with the
  shared `context-fragments` prompt contract. `RecapPromptPrefix` is Rust's new
  PROMPT_PREFIX; `RecapPromptMaxBytes` is 32768 (8,192 estimated tokens) with
  `RecapHistoryMaxBytes` reserving the instruction bytes; `RecapPrompt`
  truncates the history at a UTF-8 boundary. `RecapHistory` ports
  `recap_history.rs`: up to eight answered exchanges plus a pending correction,
  adjacent user/assistant cells merged as steering/intermediate progress, older
  whole exchanges dropped behind `[Earlier exchanges omitted]`, then both-ends
  excerpting (`[... excerpted ...]`) with a per-field share/reserve budget.
  `RecapOutputSchema` requires a `summary` (1..700) and a nullable
  `next_action` (0..200); `ParseRecap` returns `(summary, *nextAction, ok)` and
  strictly rejects unknown fields, missing keys, non-string types, and oversized
  values. `RecapDelay` is 30 minutes (was 3).
- `tui/history_cell/recap.go`: the new `  ↳ Recap: ` hanging-indent layout with
  an optional `Next:` line, the narrow-terminal header fallback, and Rust's
  oversized-token/URL re-wrap fallback.
- `tui/tea/recap.go`: builds the cell from the parsed summary + next action.
- `app/recap.go`: the temporary structured request no longer overrides
  `token_budget.use_history_notes_extension`, matching Rust #45089.
- Tests mirror Rust's new coverage: eight-exchanges-plus-pending, steering and
  intermediate progress, old-exchange omission, both-ends excerpting (three
  markers, budget margin < 8), strict parse matrix, next-action rendering, and
  the narrow URL wrap.
- Verification: `go build ./...`; `gofmt -l` clean; `go test ./tui/... ./app/
  -run ...` green (full `./tui/...` suite green, app recap tests green).
  Committed as `39a030f`.
